WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Princess Royal's Illness.—The fact that the Queen did not hasten her return from Denmark on account of the illness of the Princess Royal, is sufficient proof that Her Royal Highness's condition gave no cause for anxiety,but it has been a rather serious illness, and as in most Cases of operations for growths, there is the possibility that a second one may be necessary. Of the King's three daughters, the Queen of Norway has been always the most robust, and since her illness, not long after her marriage, the Princess has been more or less in delicate health. It might have been expected that in such circumstances the Princess would have sought health and strength at the Continental spas and resorts, but few indeed, with the means to travel, have spent so little time abroad as Her Royal Highness. Instead of going to the Riviera, or Egypt, she spends the winters at Brighton, and the summers in the country and in Scotland. The Princess has a few devoted friends, rather then a large circle of acquaintances. That is one reason why so little has been heard about her illness, which has caused the deepest regret on Deeside, and everyone hopes that Her Royal Highness will make a speedy recovery. Lord Salisbury's Sister.—Among other annoyances suffered by Lady Gwendolen Cecil in a recent libel Case was the delay of her plans for proceeding to South Africa on a visit to her brother-in-law and sister, the Earl and Countess of Selborne. Lady Gwendolen is the second daughter of the late Marquis of Salisbury, to whom she was most devoted, and scarcely ever left him after her mother's death in 1899, until he died some four years later. The late Marquis, it will be remem- bered, was left a widower, like Sir Henry Campbell- Bannerman during the period of his office as Prime Minister. To Lady Gwendolen he left his villa at Resulien, but she rarely goes to Rivera and proposes to spend some months on a visit to South Africa. Nickel Coins.—Tn France the authorities propose very shortly to substitute nickel for bronze money, and it has been suggested that we should follow their example, and abolish "coppers " in favour of smaller nickel coins. No doubt the would he a convenience in this respect, hut their resemblance to the threepenny piece and sixpence would he somewhat confusing. The clergy, at any rate, would scarcely favour such a reform, lest those of their congregation who feel constrained to give silver 4tt the collections for the sake of appear- ances, might be tempted to turn such confusion to account. _ .

----- Tradespeople and Servants.—lt is to be hoped that the payment by tr.tdespeople of "commissions " to cooks, and servants, will come within the scope of the new law, Nellie!), after this year, will make the offering of a secret commission an offence punishalsle with both flue and imprisonment. Originating in the Christmas- box system, this custom of paying "commissions," which would be more accurately described as bribes, has become so general that cooks, or other servants in a position to ialluence custom. now expect them from tradespeople, as a matter of course. In India and Chinn, it is a recognised thing that ten or fifteen per cent., or more, is added to one's household bills and accounts, which is pocketed by the native butler, and so long as the plunder is kept within recognised limits, no complaint is made by the master or mistress. In this country it is not yet regarded quite so openly, but the servants' point of view is. that their mistresses would not in any case get the "commissions," and therefore they might just as well take them as not. It . is, of course, the employer who has to pay in the long run, and it will be a good thing if the new Act does something to check this pernicious custom. Crab Apple Dar.—Long ago when the "good old customs " were observed with full honours,the maiden perplexed by many suitors for her hand used to indulge in minor observances for the purpose of ascertaining her fate, at least so history records, and one of them was the gathering on September 29, of crab apples, upon which she wrote the initials of all her eligible young men. The crabs were then put aside and not looked at again until Old Michaelmas Day. Then in the words of an old writer "Those initials which on examination are found to be most perfect, are considered to repre- sent the strongest attachment, and best for the maiden's choice." A similar experiment was the placing of onions in the chimney corner, when the first to sprout was supposed to indicate the true lover in the list of suitors. Perhaps, however, we do our ancestors an injustice in thinking that they were much given to such observances, which were of so many kinds, that they could not possibly all giveethe same result, and it would certainly seem hard noon the true lover to have her fate decided by the accident of a sprouting onion, or decaying crab

apple. The Summer War lrobe.—The time has arrived when all summer garments must he over-hauled, with a view, to ascertaining what is, and what is not, worth keeping. Shirts, linen blouses, muslin blouses, skirts, and under- skirts, and indeed all garments that are about to be laid on one side until next summer, and can be washed, should then be thoroughly dried, and packed away in their rough condition. Articles of clothing should never be- starched and dressed, "{then about to be laid by for onv length of time. Gowns that need to pay a visit to the cleaners, should do so at once, and then be carefully packed away in many sheets of tissue paper. others that require only a good brushing, and wiping down with clean soft white rags, should promptly receive such treatment, moreover braids,sand skirt frills should be looked to and repaired. It is little short of marvell- ons, the difference such care makes in the length of wear, and appearance of n woman's clothes. Velveteena.—Autumn modes are now full upon'us,and we are well aware that it is to be a velvet season. Not all among us. can however afford this rich and beautiful fabric. but even so we need not despair, there are left the velveteens. The new velveteens are wonderftry rich and silky in effect, and promise to wear splendidly:\ especially the corduroy variety. In price they are even MOTO moderate than they were a year ago, and, simply strapped with the same material, a velveteen coat and skirt should not prove a heavy outlay—and while touch- ing the subject of velveteen coats. it is an item worth remembering, that the sac shape is the one to be pre- ferred. Fitted coats of velveteen are not often a success. About Pockets.—With the new autumn skirts we were promised pockets, but so far they have not arrived

"A PINE FOREST IN EVERY HOME."

The New Treatment for Coughs, Colds,

and Bronchitis.

!

The greatest discoveries have come from the observa- tion of little things. Newton discovered gravitation through seeing an apple fall from a tree. Franklin made his famous electrical discovery by going out in a thunder storm with a kite.

Modern scientists have noticed the singular freedom of the Swiss peasants dwelling in the midst of the rich pine forests of the Alps from lung and chest disease, and have been driven to the conclusion that in certain species of pine is hidden a medicinal agency of great value in preventing or curing coughs, colds, bronchitis, and kindred troubles.

When a 'similar robustness was observed among the dwellers in the pine-woods all over Europe and America this conviction was deepened. Although the value of the pine-air to the weak-chested and consumptive has been long recognised, it is only recently that the medicinal properties of the pine have been fully understood.

A small, pleasant-tasting tablet has been invented which is a wonderful concentration of these rich pine extracts. As the tablet is crushed or dissolved on the i tongue the pleasant pine essences are set free on their i errand of health. In combination with the valuable pine extracts are certain ether substances of great assistance in lung and chest complaints. I These peps (as the tablets are called) enjoy the ' distinction of being unique, and also the most powerful and natural remedy for colds, chills, influenza, sore throat, bronchitis, and all lung and throat troubles. I They relieve the hacking cough, and prevent lung- weakness after pleurisy or pneumonia. Children with croup, cold or whooping cough derive the utmost I benefit from Peps, which have the additional advantage of beirv, free from opium and all harmful drugs. To have Peps handy is equal to having • A Pine Forest in your own home," the aim being to confer in a less costly, more speedy and more convenient form ' the same benefits that come to the wealthy invalid I from treatment in the rich pine woods of Switzerland and Norway. When little Pep (divested of the silver paper in which it is sealed and preserved) is crushed in the • month, or merely placed on the tongue and allowed to dissolve, a grateful, soothing sensation is felt through- out the mouth and down the throat into the lungs. All the genuine pine-essences which have been hold captive in the tablet are released, and permitted to bathe the ' tissues of the throat and lungs as the breath is taken in. The fumes impregnate every inch of membrane, front the mouth and nostrils to the furthest part of the I respiratory organs. i A germicide and antiseptic of the highest value is thus brought into direct contact with the diseased sarfaces of tie throat and lungs, and the pleasant sense- -8100 aroused without is evidence of the work of healing begun within. 1 While- liquid cough mixtures are merely swallowed I into the stomach, Peps soothe and heal the actual air- ' passages of the lungs and bronchial tubes, which are the seat of disease. Peps promote sound health by repairing the membranes that have been torn by the , 'wheezing and barking of Bronchitis, and they help to allay the hacking cough of Consumption. I As a household remedy Peps are of uneqnalled service especially in cases of colds, coughs, bronchial affections, sore or relaxed throat, huskiness, loss of voice, asthma, I influenza, pneumonia, pleurisy, that hacking cough, I lung-weakness, children's colds, whooping cough, croup, I chills, and tightness of the chest. Clergymen, lawyers, teachers' singers, and public speakers will find Peps invaluable for the voice and throat. Price Is. lid. per box, of all chemists and stores ; or post free from the sole proprietors, The Peps Pastille Co., Carlton Hill, Leeds. Every mother needs Peps. PENKETH.

FRIENDS' Scwoot.—Mr. George E. Thompson, of Liverpool gavean interesting lecture the other day at the boy's school on " Moorish Spain," illustrated by limelight views. Visitors were invited from Penketh, Sankey and Warrington, and there was an excellent attendance. Mr. Thompson dealt ably and exhaustively

' with his subject, conducting his hehrers on a tour through the older and historical parts of Spain. Mr. Brown briefly proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Thomp- son, remarking how much everyone appreciated his lectures, and added that it was quite unnecessary for him to say anything, as Mr. Thompson was now so well known at the school that the pleasure of the audience was self-evident. The motion was seconded and carried. Masses of them collect to form thrombi, and this may occur in an injured ves- sel, which may be thus plugged up. Hence Dekhuyzen has suggested that the blood plate- lets may act as special agents to protect against accidental hmmorrhage. - Bezzozero contends that they are definite mor- phological elements in living, unmixed blood. This conclusion rests, however, on the evidence of only a few observations, and other observers deny its accuracy. The difficulty lies largely in the fact that the usual method of demonstrat- ing. their presence, is by mixing shed blood with various reagents, and it is thought that they are the results of consequent disintegration. When shed blood is protected from contact with glass, and sheltered from evaporation, few or no blood-platelets are found. And when they have been observed in the veins there has always been some injury. SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS.'

A NEW CONSUMPTION REMEDY.

Palladium chloride is said to be the best re- medy kit the trestmens of consumption, its ad. ministration being followed by improvement of the appetite of the patieat, the disappearancO of the fever, and cessation of colghing. It is, how. over, not to be used in nervous or neurasthenic patients. A 3 per cent. solution is employed, be- ing administered in five to ton-drop doses before :u3a.13.

Professor ilfarigliano declares that science now possesses specific means of attacking consump- tion, but that the means can only be useful if they are employed before the infection has ravaged the tissues, and while the organisation of the patient is in a condition to profit by them. They constantly remain fruitless when destruct- ivo lesions have been produced, or have even begun to be produced.

===== 0002601_19060929_art0010.txt | included=true | included =====
COMPOSITION OF METEORS.

The meteors that flash across the sky at cer. tz...in yearly -periods usually burn out in the upper air, but occasionally a meteoric TIMM lasts long enough to reach the earth. One fell on May lbth, 1900, at Felix, in the State of Alabama. This meteorite was analysed, and found to bo built up of such minerals as olivine, augite, triolito, nickel-iron( and graphite car- bon. The dark colour of the Felix stone is stated to be due to the presence in fair amount of the last-named substance. The interest at- taehing to meteorites, of course, centres around the fact that, they enable us to pbtain glimpses ol the composition of other •worlds than ours. i Astronomy s well agreed on the unity of chemical composition which marks the orbs, asd the fact that hydrogen gas in the sun gives us light and heat iii a testimony to this fact.

SULPHUR'-WORKING IN LOUISIANA. Sulphur deposits of considerable value occur is the State of Louisiana. Great difficulty was at first experienced in dealing with these deposits, 'which occur at a depth of between 600 it. and BCoft. below the surface, by reason of the fact that the overlying strata contained quicksand. The beds have been ascertained, to be between Waft. and 200 ft. thick. and extend for many miles underground. They are now being mined by means of steam and compressed air. Borings are driven into the stratum of sulphur, and a steam pipe leads down to the bottom of the bore. Steam is then forced down the pipe, and the melted sulphur is brought to the surface by a compressed air lift-pump system. At the surface it is discharged into large concrete-faced shallow vats or pools, where it is allowed to solidify, after which it is broken up and shipped to the re- finery or direct to the market.

===== 0002601_19060929_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
INSECTS AND THEIR ODOURS.

Butterflies have colours, so the evolutionists say, in order to attract attention; it is believed by some entomologists .hat they possess perfume in order to attract one another. Such perfumes aro called epigamic, because they are usually characteristio of the male butterflies, and are held to attract the female. Mr. F. A. Dixey, of Wadham College, detected some years are an exhalation from certain butterflies resembling. in odour letnori verbena. The odour is characteristic of a well-known white butterfly; and some Indian species ad European forms have lately been added to the list. Mr. Dixey now an- nounces the discovery of similar odours, resem- bling aromatic vegetable products like chocolate or vanilla, and in some casee flower scents, in a larg4 number of South African species. In addi- tion to these agreeable perfumes are others, of- fensive in character, and common to both sexes, which are presumably designed to repel the at- tentions of birds which prey on butterflies. The attraetive scents are distributed by special scales, and the exudations seem to be partially under the control of the butterflies. The offensive scents are stronger and seem to characterise .the whole body.

COPPER CELLULOSE FOR GAS-MANTLES.

So far all attempts made to replace the threads. of cotton or ramie, out of which the mantles are made, by other materials—silk, jute, and arti- f-cial threads—have failed. Considerable success appears now, however, says Enginccring, to have been obtained with threads of copper cellulose. Certain modifications in the manufacture of the mantles were required to render the innovation useful. The cotton fibre of the common mantles is impregnated with a solution of nitrate of thorium, containing the small amount of eerie mixed with the thoria. This nitrate swells con- siderably when it is heated; afterwards, during calcination to the oxide thoria, it shrinks, and this is not a desirable feiture. The copper cellu- lose thread also absorbs a solution of thorium nitrate. But the fibre afterwards, on calcination, Crilthbles to a powder. A Spaniard, Plaissetty; chewed how this difficulty could be overcome. When an alkaline solution of thorium nitrate is precipitated with ammonia, a gelatinous thorium hydrate falls out, which does 'not swell on heat- ing, and which shrinks to small crystals, almost ,as hard as diamonds, during calcination. When the precipitation of the nitrate is effected in the impregnated fibre of copper cellulose, very dur- able mantles are obtained. Some remarkable ad- vantages are claimed for the new copper cellulose mantles. The common mantles are hygroscopic, and have, for this as for other reasons, to be packed singly in hermetically-sealed boxes. The new mantles are not hygroscopic, and may be soaked in water for hours. Still more important is the high mechanical strength claimed for the new mantles.

===== 0002601_19060929_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NATURE OF RADIUM.

Writing to the Times on the raditim contro- versy. Sir Oliver Lodge says: " The key of the position is the elementary or the compound character of radium. Nearly all physicists, following Rutherford and Soddy in the first instance,, now consider it to be a dis- integrating element displaying a novel form of energy: Lord Kelvin and a few chemists enlist- ing under his banner pronounce it an explosively decomposing compound. All agree that it must . be being born' somehow, because of its com- paratively short life: but whereas the experi- menters assert that they know its parentage, Lord Kelvin tl;tlts them too definite in that re- spect. There remains an Outstanding difference with the Hon. R. J. Strutt about the source of I the present heat in the crust of the earth; and that is about all. The controversy about the ele- mentary or the compound nature of the radium atom is not one that need be Settled offhand. It does not really depend on the use of terms; there will be no need for chemists to alter the use of the convenient names of • element' and 'atom' because their etymology has become archaic, even when the physicists ',have turned out com- pletely right. If radium splits up, as it does, it must in some sense be compound; and yet in every respect it behaves as much like an ele- ment, of the calciumstrontium-barium series, as any other element whose spectrum, atomic weight, and family relationship are known. t Moreover, I believe that its instability, compared with that of other elements, is only a .question .of degree, not of kind; for radioactivity has been provisionally shev:n in the Cavendish Laboratory to be a property of nearly all matter."

WOMAN'S REALM.

DRESS -FASHION-HOME.

A CLEVER LADY MUSICIAN.

• Mrs. W. J. Morrow, of Peterborough, Ontario, Canada, who has been in London for tho last three months attending the National College of Music, has been successful in passing the Fellow- ship Examination with distinction. In addition, she is a professor of the National College—s posi- tion her great abilities will enable her well to ful- fil. It is rarely that a lady succeeds in passing the Fellowship Examination, for the tests are very severe; but Mrs. Morrow displayed know- ledge and ability seldom met with. Her many friends and admirers in Peterborough and else- where in Canada and the States have.received the news of her success with much pleasure, and a hearty reception awaits this lady on her re-. turn. She has long held a high position in the musical world both in Canada and. the United States, and is a Gold Medallist

COAT AND SKIRT FOR AIITGMN.

The illustration shews a well-cut and quite plain little coat and skkt intended for autumn wear, and realised in a soft, old rose face cloth., The little short coat is many-seamed at the back, and is cut away in front. It fastens across with three small tabs, and is finished with a collar faced with black velvet. wnere they end in a little silk kilted frill. The plain skirt is hemmed with a band of black velvet, while the hat is en suite," being of soft bendable felt, in the same shade pf old rose as the costume, and is trinuped with black velvet ribbon and a bunch of black quills.

STRIPED GAUZES.

The striped gawks and chiffons are particu- larly pretty, and there is a pronounced fancy for such semi-transparent striped effects in contrasting weaves or fabric, but in one tone. The loveliest effects are obtained in grouped stripes of tulle and satin, crêpe and silk mousse- line, chiffon and net. With voile any trimming seems consistent, and it is difficult to decide which makes the most attractive combination—cloth, silk, satin, or velvet. For the autumn velvet perhaps looks warmer and richer, not necessarily the heavy velvet, but one of the finer grade, as panne or chiffon velvet. With a wide belt the velvet should also be introduced in the trimming on both blouse and skirt. Even a little touch on the sleeves will bring out well the tone of the voile. _ ••

• A shrimp-pink cape-de-chine gown had deep bands of brown velvet round the skirt, and touches of velvet were introduced successfully on the bodice. The embroidered tulles and nets will be lavishly used as trimming, being inttoduced even upon faced cloths and the heavy silks.

k'HE PICTURESQUE CRAZE.

Once more English women are beginning to realise how much better they look when clad in picturesque modes rather than those smart chic fashions so becoming to their Parisian sisters. Already, says Mrs. Hugh Adams in London Opinion, one notices the return of the picture hat, very broad in the brim, trimmed with long floating feathers and gracefully drooping plumes. These are mostly made in light straws of pastel colouring, and trimmed with black or coloured feathers. A particularly beautiful example is a pale golden Leghorn hat ornamented with pale ' blue velvet ribbons and great clusters of purple and white grapes arranged over the fiat crown. FROM. FOR SMALL CHILD.

The, little girl's frock which forms thee sub- ject of our present sketch was taken from a particularly pleasing model. It could be realised in almost any material, but for the coming months nothing could be prettier, more ser- viceable. and less expensive than white Scotch winsey, of which material quite a nice quality,

Zan. wide, can be obtained for Is. per yaw% The yoke is formed of silk embroidery worked on the winsey, and the straps of embroidery are continued down the back as well as down the front. The skirt is nkie and full, and is finished with an embroidered frill. The sleeves in our model are short, but for cold weather most people prefer to clothe their children in long- sfeeved frocks, except for such special occasions as parties. THE PLUCKIEST WOMAN IN THE WORLD.

Madame Camille du Cast, the intrepid French. woman, is tall, but powerfully built, with a womanly face wreathed in a perpetual smile. She vvearsi pince-nez, for her eyes are the weakest part about her. She also wears, when occasion demands, the bifurcated garb of manhood. This wonderful woman—who has just started on an official visit to Morocco—is, without doubt, the pluckiest woman in the world. Her nerves, if such uncertain things play any part in her phy- eiology, must be of steel, foT she is afraid of nc. thing. Into her thirty-six years of life she has crammed more adventure than usually galls to the lot of 100 men, and yet her late husbind's oe cupation was nothing more romantic than a house furnisher on the hire system. A new sen- sation, to appeal to Madame, must be dangerous. She has done everything that sport or science hal thought of up to the present time

CHAPTER XVIII. WHEN Miss [Miggs and Daisy Graham left! thel Cafe in Bishops' Road, Bayswater, they took an omnibus which would drop them near the British Museum and qiute close to the street where the little old lady lodged. She had already made plans in her swiftly working mind for Daisy's immediate future. Although Miss IMiggs was an idealist and a bit of a poetess in her way, she was an eminently practical woman. The more she thought of Daisy's case the more sure she felt that the girl was absolutely innocent. No one could •talk to Daisy Graham for five minutes without being im- pressed with the charm of her simplicity and absolute sincerity. One would as soon imagine an angel capable of a mean action as Daisy, and in shielding her Miss Miggs felt that she had done one of the best of the many good actions of her life.

with. I want you to dress differently from what you do now simply because your other aunt will no doubt have circulated a descrip- tion of you and you will inevitabli be caught and brought back to her. That felt hat is very pretty and stylish, but I am going to put it on the fire; at least, I'll put it in my ward- robe till the time for fires comes round again ; also that coat which I will give to some poor person. Now if you will wait for me for ten minutes I will see what I can do in the way of disguise." She went out and returned in about twenty minutes with a bonnet box alio a parcel. In the bonnet box was a very becoming chip hat, at least it looked very becoming when Daisy tried it on. In the parcel was a little blue serge coat which almost exactly matched her skirt. When the girl tried them on her appearance was completely changed from what one would have figured from the description of her which was circulated in all the papers. "But," said Daisy, as she looked at herself ,in the glass, " the expense, how can I—" " Don't say anything about it," said Miss Miggs. "You can pay me afterwards when you have money. lam financing you—that is the correct term I believe—for the next few months, and now, if you are ready, we will go Out, for I have some business to do."

She also felt distinctly that Providence had sent her to save Daisy, and indeed the most hard-headed person could not but admit that her intervention had been providential. When they arrived in Street Miss Miggs paused before a somewhat gloomy look- ing house, and drawing the latch key from her pocket opened the door. " I have the whole top i of the house to my- self," said the old lady, as she tripped up the stairs before her guest. "It is so much more quiet. I hate having people over my head. If there's one thing that drives me frantic its noises over head."

The old lady looked into her purse to see if she had enough money, then she wrapped some crusts from the breakfast table in a piece of newspaper and placed them in the bag she always carried, then she led the way down- stairs, Daisy dimly wondering whether the crusts were destined for poor people or their own luncheon.

She opened a door and led Daisy into a sitting-room where the gas was half turned up. It was a pleasant room, and though very plainly furnished, 'there were aplenty of books about and some flowers in a vase on the table. Over the fireplace was a large photograph of the Winter Palace at St. Petersberg, and on the walls affjoining were photographs of Loris Melikoff and Anton Strudivitch, who tried to blow the Winter Palace up. Everybody has their weak points. The Czar, as I have hinted before, was Miss Miggs'. Now, dear," said she, as she turned the gas up, "make yourself at home, sit down in that easy chair; there is a bedroom upstairs simply waiting to be occupied; the sheets are aired, they always are, for I never know who'll be corning to stop with me." " I can never forget or repay your kind- ness," said Daisy, "not if I live to be a hun- dred."

They were, as a- matter of fact, for the pigeons of the British Museum which Daisy's companion, it appears, always fed of a morn- ing. This important business accomplished, they turned their steps towards Oxford Street.

CHAPTER XX.

ARTHUR 'ESMOND, on finding himself free from prison, made his way home to his lodg- ings in Bloomsbury. _

By a strange chance the house where he lived was within a very short walk of Miss Miggs' residence where at that moment Daisy was staying.

But ai Very short walk may mean eternal separation in London. One might live in London in the same street with a person for a year and not see ithern once, and to be lost in London is like being lost in the Atlantic Ocean.

"Oh, I hope you won't 'live to be that," said Miss Miggs cheerfully ;- "a hundred is much too old, dear, for people to go on living at, and now that we're here and comfortable let us talk about things." They talked for an hour or more, Miss Miggs doing most of the talking; her one endeavour seemed to be to draw the girl's mind away from herself, and she succeeded.

The horror Of his own position was nothing to Arthur compared to his despair at Daisy's action.

At half-past ten she led the way upstairs and showed Daisy her loom. It was a small, bright little room as clean as a new pin, fur- nished with a, camp bed, with a snow-white coverlet and some birch-wood bedroom furni- ture.

" Ivan Ivanovitch slept in that bed once," said the old lady as she bade the girl good- night. " Ivanovitch, the great Russian patriot."

"Dear me," said Daisy. "Yes, they hanged him. It was partly his fault; he would go back to Russia, and they caught him and hanged him. Good-night, dear, and pleasant dreams." "Good-night," said Daisy, with a little shiver inspired lw 6.k.e story of Ivan Ivano- vitch. . Thet scarcely had the .old lady closed the door than she opened it again. !!"Do you like sausages ? " she asked.

"Yes" replied Dar.sy somewhat startled at the irrelevancy of the question. "Then we'll have them for breakfast," said her hostess, and closed the door for good. Daisy laughed, then her eyes filled with tears. A few hours before she had been a‘ homeless wandeier in London, now she had found a protector and a roof to shelter her head. She stood for a moment with her hands clasped, then she knelt down beside the little snow-white bed to offer thanks for the great mercy of it all.

Miss Princeps looked at him almost kindly; the trouble she had gone through since Daisy's flight, the knowledge that was suddenly borne in upon her that she had treated the girl with unmerited severity and coldness, the suspense and the fear that her niece had com- mitted suicide, all this had made a great change in her in a few hours. " I will let you know immediately I hear anything," she replied. Then he took his de- parture.

At three o'clock in the afternoon of that day he was in Oxford Street near Mudie's Library. Without occupation now, for the bank had suspended him pending a full investigation of the case and the magistrate's decision; he was without an object or a business in life, and he stood on the pavement debating in his own mind as to how he should kill the time. He remembered the instructions of Lord de Mor- ley as to keeping his eyes open on the chance cif seeing again the man who had robbed him. But it seemed a hopeless sort of quest. He wandered away from Oxford Street through the maze of thoroughfares around the British Museum, walking listlessly and not caring where he went; he turned a corner into Bloomsbury Street. At the third house from the corner on the doorstep stood two ladies. One of them had just taken a latch key from her pocket to open the door. It was Miss Miggs ; beside her stood Daisy Graham.

Arthur stopped and they turned. He was only three paces from them. LADY'S TERRIBLE DEATH.

MNS Henrietta Davies, aged thirty, daughter of the late Mr. Ogleby Davies, and niece of the well-known philanthropist and coalowner, Mr. John Cory, met with a tragic death on Tuesday morning. Accompanied by Mrs. Vivian, a widow, she was walking along the Penarth cliffes to view the Bristol Channel boat race. In her excitement she stepped too near the edge, when. missing her footing, she fell headlong down to the beach 120 feet below. Death was instantaneous.

An unusual incident occurred during a run by the Puckeridge Hunt whilst, cub hunting on Tuesday. The pack were scenting Clavering Park, Saffron Walden, when they came across a badger. He was a fine fellow, and showed a good fight, but after a desperate struggle the animal was killed by the hounds.

CHAPTER XIX.

CNEXT morning after breakfast Daisy's new friend began to put her plans into operation. , She knew that a vivid description of Daisy was by this time in the hands of every detec- tive in London and that, therefore, great care was necessary. During the night she had been :greatly exercised in her mind as tO whether she ought to tell Daisy the whole posi- tion of affairs and of the fact that a warrant Iwas out at that moment for her arrest, An ordinary person would have done so, but Miss IMiggs was not an ordinary person by any , means. 1 "If I tell her," thought she, " she will at once go and give herself up; they will put her in prison as a matter of course, and prison to a girl like that would be a worse experience than death. As far as I Can see two people only are responsible for her position. Her wicked old fool of an aunt and that man Arthur Esmond. They've done this to her; well, let them be content, they shan't have her back. I God has given her into my hands to keep, and I I'll keep her. I'll adopt her if necessary, and any little money I have to leave after my death I'll leave her." ! In taking this resolution she well knew that , she was committing herself to a very serious course of action. Noti only was she cheating the law of its prey] and society of a victim, but she was hiding from Daisy the; knowledge that an'accusation was hanging over her of a serious I I nature. She determined, however, to let the , accusation hang; if the girl had possessed a 1 family it would have been different, but she I was an orphan, and the disgrace of the affair Iwould fall only on the aunt who had always ill- used her and who, In Miss Miggs' opinion, t deserved disgrace. Now tell me," she said after breakfast, I"do you really and truly wish to begin the world for yourself with my aid? " I " I do,' said Daisy. 'I am willing to do ; any work no matter how humble so long as I I can earn an independence." " Do you ever wish to see your aunt again?" "I do not wish to speak bitterly about ; her," said the girl, "but I do not wish to see I her again—never again." I "You wish to have done entirely with your past life. The-re is no one whom you have ever met you would care.to Meet again ? " tsN Daisy 'blushed; she was thinking of Arthur Esmond. Then her eyes sparkled with anger as she recalled again for the fiftieth • time since • yesterday how he had given up her letter, as she thought, wrongfully. I -" No one," he answered firmly: t I "You have no objection to changing your 1 name ? " - rit , None, I would much prefer to do so. I would like to have done with the past alto- gether and forget it if that were possible." I "So would many peopre in this weary world," said the old lady." but it is not possi- ble, it is the law of life that we cannot foresee the future and that we cannot forget the past. 1 Well, now to be practical, what name will you assume? I once had a very dear niece who is I dead. Her • name was Winifred Grey. Will ,you ,take that name and be my niece—also in 1 Ilame ? " . "Gladly," said Daisy. "It is a beautiful name."

"Very well then, Winifred," said Miss Miggs, "you are now my niece and I shall exercise the authority of an aunt. To begin

===== 0002601_19060929_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
me at thi least explain." The girl looked at him for one moment with an expression of contempt on her face that froze the words upon his tongue, then, taking the latch key from her companion, she opened the door and entered the house, leaving Miss Miggs to follow if she pleased. Never was a misunderstanding between lovers so curious and so disastrous. He imagined naturally that she knew of the disas- ter that had befallen him and that part at least of her contempt was due to it. " She thinks I am a robber," thought the unfortunate young man. "She is against me with all the rest of the world. She might, at least, have waited until I was proved guilty." He turned from the door with a deathly bitterness in his heart. , The whole world seemed to have conspired to ruin him. "What have I done? "he thought as he walked away, "to be robbed of position, honour, and love all in one day? I have never hurt a human being. Why, then, should I have been punished like this ? " _ _

He had not gone many steps when he felt a light touch on his arm, and turning found himself face to face with Miss Miggs. " Excuse me," said the old lady, "but you are Mr. Esmond, I believe ? " ____

"That is my name," answered Arthur. " You are—you were a friend of Miss Graham's ? "

Arthur laughed bitterly. "Please don't talk of friendship," he said, " there is no such thing in this world." "Oh yes there is," said his interrogator, " and there is such a thing as love. Now, Mr. Esmond, if you are a friend of Miss Graham's you will prove your friendship by saying nothing to anyone of this meeting with her. She wishes to have done with her past life; she has changed her name, and is living with me. Promise me to say nothing about this meet- ing." . _ .

. I will say nothing," answered Arthur, "but considering all the circumstances of the case it would have been‘ better for Daisy—l mead Miss Graham—if instead of runnilig away she had explained matters. It is nBt too late still "

" Yes," replied Miss Miggs irritably, " it is not too late still for her to be put in prison. It is for you, sir, to explain matters and to re- move the imputation that hangs over you and her. I have thought the whole matter out and I have taken Miss Graham's future into my keeping, and if I can help it she is not going to be put into a police cell and dragged before a police magistrate and tortured and ruined mentally for life. She's safe with me. I'm Miss Miggs, and she's not the first victim I've saved from that fiend, the Law, and I hope she won't bel the last." She did not tell Arthur that Daisy was ignorant that any imputation was hanging over her or him. The law to Miss Miggs was a beast that devoured innocent and guilty alike. and she was uttetly determined that Daisy should not put a foot in its den. So they parted. And Arthur returned to his lodgings with the knowledge of his sweet- heart's safety. Though he imagined that she had treated him badly and taken sides against him, the knowledge that she was safe and not lost for ever in the great whirl of Londoi. brought him intense relief. He felt no anger against her for the bitter

words she had a-.ddressed to him, only an in- tense grief. Though she had slighted him and treated him with scorn, she had not destroyed in the least his affection for her.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES. Organ Recital. After the evening service at Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church on Sunday, an orga4 recital was given by Master T. W. Pearce, the church organist, and vocal items were given by Mr. E. H. Pearce (bass). Master Pearce ably rendered " Sonato No. 3 " (Mendelssohn, " Andantino " (Semare) " Grand chorus in D Major ' Guilinant), and" Fantasia in G Minor " (The Storm) (Lemmens), his rendering of the last-named being par- ticularly effective. Mr. Pearce sang "Why do the nations ? (Handel) and" Calvary "(Paul Rodnby) in an excellent manner. Mr Carnegie's Gift to Bold-street Church. An interesting announcement was made by the Rev. J. MANNING at the close of Sunday evening's service at Bold-street Wesleyan Church. He had that morning, he said, received a letter from Mr. Andrew Carnegie promising to defray half the cost of a new organ to be erected in the church at an outlay of £B6O. He would forward his cheque when the congregation had raised their half of the sum required. It was hoped between now and May, Mr. Manning added, to clear off the debt on the church premises of £400,t0 renovate the interior of the church, and provide the new organ, the total ex- penditure involved being £1,600. A bazaar would be held in May with the object of raising £l,OOO. Mr. Carnegie's offer has been accepted. St. Paul's Church.

(Uopyrsglit.)

THE RYL OF THE LILIES.

By L FRANK BAUM,

Author of "Father Goose: His Book," "A New Wonderland," "The Wonderful Wizard ol Oz,"

===== 0002601_19060929_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
Saturday, September 29, 1906.

hunted for high and low and cannot find. It is the only plant-food in the world that will dye the tips of the stamens of my lilies that rich yellow colour which is so greatly admired and which offers such a beautiful contrast to the pure white of the leaves. There are hosts of lilies to be supplied with this colouring. A month or so ago I found enough of the food in Senegambia to colour all my lilies except that one big field in Brasil; but I fear those must now remain pure white since no more colour-food is to be had."

"It's too bad," said Bob, sympathetically. "I wish I could help you." The ryl clapped his hands with delight. "Perhaps you can," he said, with a sly wink at the boy. "Why do you suppose I have been stopping here with you and chattering nonsense unless I had a purpose? Ryls are not allowed to ask favours of human beings, but since you offer to help me of your own free will, why, -there is no reason I should not accept the kindness." "What can I do ? " asked the boy, wonderingly. "Your grandfather once had a bottle of the very plant-food lam in search of," replied the ryl. "I don't know where he got it or what he used it for, but he kept it in a tall blue bottle in the cupboard over the chimney." "Oh, yes," said Bob. "I've seen the bottle often."

"Do you know where it is now ? " asked the ryl. "It is still in the cupboard over the chimney. Mother has never touched it since grandpa died, for she didn't know what it was." The little man rsse to his feet and leant over the edge of the stump as he said, in a voice that trembled with anxiety. "If I had that bottle I should be happy t" "Then I'll get it for you," replied Bob. "But if yon wanted it so badly, why didn't you go and take it ? " _

"That would be stealing," answered the ryl. "But, if you make me a present of your grand- father's bottle you will save my beautiful field of Brazilian lilies."

"I'll run for it now," cried the boy. "Wait here until I C'ome back."

I— He sprang up end darted away towards the I village. When he reached it he tore down the main street at full speed and reached" his home almost out of breath. His mother happened to be out at the time, but the door was unlatched, and Bob soon pushed a chair to the chimney and began searching in the high cupboard for the blue bottle. It stood in the exact spot his grandfather had 1 let it, and seizing it in his hand the boy climbed I down rnd began his return journey without loss of time. When he reached the North Fields there was the ryl, sitting upon the stump and railing patiently. Bob placed the bottle on the stump beside the little fellow and noticed, with surprise, that the cork reached far above the ryl's head. "He'll never be able to open it," thought the boy; but the ryl did not seem a bit worried. He placed his hand over his heart and bowed very gravely to Bob, saying: "I cannot tell you how grateful I am for this favour. You have removed all my troubles by means of your gift. Now, in return, tell me why you were crying when I found you here." #, "I had hoped to gather some wild-flowers for Easter," said Bob; 'but when I got here I found they had not yet blossomed. We are too poor to keep a flower garden, you know; so when I found there were no wild flowers, it made me very sorry." The ryl leant against the blue bottle, thrust his hands deep in his trousers pockets, and whistled a tune thoughtfully. The boy watched him with much anxiety, for he had begun to hope the ryl would help him. By-and-bye, the little man said: "Well, you must come wjth me to Brazil. That is the only way to help you. When you are there, I'll give you enough lilies to make you the wonder and admiration of the whole village." "Oh, I can't go so far from home!" cried the boy. "Mother would be dreadfully worried about we. _ _ _ J.

The ryl laughed. "It's a far journey," he said, "but not a long one. Here—take this bottle in your left hand." Bob obeyed, although he was somewhat afraid. "Now give me the little finger of your other hand," continued the ryl. Bob held it out, and the man closed his tiny fingers about the boy's bigger one in a firm clasp. "Here we go!" remarked the ryl, calmly, and Bob gave a great start of surprise and amazement. For the North Fields, the stump and the familiar landscape had entirely disappeared, and the boy found himself standing beside the ryl in the midst of a vast field of pure white lilies, which nodded I their heads in the tropical breeze, as if welcoming I him among them. While he admired their glorious beauty, the voice of the ryl reached his ears. "If I don't enlarge myself, I may get stepped on. Watch me grow!" Bob looked down and saw the top of the little man's head growing gradually bigger, and rising higher and higher until the ryl had become fully as tall as the boy. "Now I feel safer," he said; "and if you will please hand me the bottle, I'll get to work and feed my flowers." Bob gave him the blue bottle, and, removing the cork, the ryl began to sprinkle its contents on the earth at the roots of the lilies. He worked very quickly and carefully, and the colour-food lasted until the furthest part of the field had been fed. Then, the bottle being empty, the ryl returned to where Bob was standing, and said: "See how quickly my lilies have gained their yellow-tipped stamens. Sure enough, when Bob peeped among the leaves, he saw little caps of brilliant yellow swayieg the slender stems that supported them. "My work is now complete," continued the rp, joyously; "and as I owe my success to your liindnese I shall be glad to have you gather as many of my lilies as you can carry home. Be sure to pick long stems, for then the flowers will remain fresh for several days." The! boy did not hesitate to accept this generous offer, and soon he had gathered a great , armful of the exquisite flowers.

"These will (It; thank you," said he. But the ryl was not content. " Wait—wait—wait ! " he cried, laughingly, and, plucking more and more of the flowers, he fairly loaded Bob down with them. Then, when the boy's eyes could just see over the top of his lilies, the ryl cried out: "Now, then—hold tight! Good-bye—there you go!" "Good-bye!" answered 'Bob; but his voice sounded strangely far away in his ears, and in the same instant he was standing in the North Fields, his arms filled with great bunches of beautiful Easter lilies, which filled the air with their delicate fragrance.

===== 0002601_19060929_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
111.

The little village church was gorgeously arrayed that Easter Sunday, for Bob's lilies were on every side, and were the wonder of all who saw them. Never before had such exquisite flowers been brought to • the town, and everyone inquired where they came from. _ _ _

So Bob's story was soon well known, for he haA told his mother and the minister all about his adventure with the little ryl. After the sermon was over, the widow approached the minister, and asked:

"What do you think about it, sir? Did my boy really meet with this creature be calls a ryl ?" "Well," answered . the minister, gravely, "it sounds to our ears like a very improbable tale. But if it is not true where did the lilies come from ? "

"They certainly never grew in the North Fields," replied the widow. "Perhaps, sir, a miracle has come to pass !" "Quite likely, madam," said the good man; "Easter is the season of miracles."

[Played on the ground of the club ruasked*.] WARRING [ON V. WIDNES.

Warrington were at home to Widnes on Saturday, and a flue crowd of 6,000 people witnessed a fast and excit- ing game in flue weather. The home side was short of Isherwood and Fish, but their places were well-filled by Dickenson and Harris. The visitors were well repre- sented. Teams :

Warrington—Berry, back; Harris, Dickinson, Taylor and Brooks, three-quarter backs; Hockenhall and Lees, half-backs; Preston. Boardman, Thomas, Heath, Naylor, and liarmer, forwards. Widnes—Tilley, back ; Lambert, Davies, J. Taylor and Barber three-quarter back; Lally and Noon, half backs ; S. Aspey, G. Aspey, Carr, Turner, Argent and Rigby, forwards. RefereA, Mr. J. H. Crompton, Bradford. Touch judges, Messrs. H. Davies (Faton)and G. Robinson (Runcorn). Preston started. Eventually good forward work found Widnes showing to distinct advantage, and after , Barber had showed up well Widnes came near scoring through Lally. They continued to attack, and Harris failing to play the ball. Tilley from the ensuing penalty placed a goal. Warrington forced the pace after this, and Hockenhall taking a sharp pass from Lees, scored in a position for Preston to add the goal. Soon after Brooks was held up at the corner, and a sequence of fast transfers led up to Harris scoring at tho left hand corner. Half-time: Warrington 8 points, Widnes 2 points. Turning round Widnes were soon in difficultiee, and a fine piece of combination between Hockenhall, Lees, Taylor, and Brooks ended in a try to the last named. Preston added the goal. Warrington con- tinued to show clever football, and passing between Hockenhall and Harris ended in the last-named scoring. Preston added another goal. Immediately after Widnes I were penalised and Preston added a penalty goal. Widnes made spasmodic bursts, but Berry proved rife, and a fine movement by the Warrington forwards resulted in a try to Harmer, Preston adding the fourth goal. Ike Taylor and Boardman also scored tries one of which Berry converted. Result :—Warrington 5 ' goals 7 tries (31 points), Widnes 1 goal (2 points).

RUNCORN WIN AT LEIGH. Runcorn and Leigh met at Leigh before 3,000 people. The home side was handicapped by the absence of injured players, and had to bow the knee to the Linnets." Leigh attacked at the outset, but a penalty drove them back. Play was in the Leigh quarter when Whittaker tried to throw to Bennett, but Runcorn inter- cepted and rushed to the line. A scrummage ensued, and the ball was passed out to Flanagan. who scored at the corner, Jolley placing a grand goal. The run of play after this was in favour of Leigh, but they were lacking in finish. The most brilliant run of the day was made by S. Johnson, who got the ball at the centre, kicked, and followed up, and after dodging several men tipped the leather over the line, but before he could reach it Jolley touched down. The Leigh forwards twice rushed the ball over the line, but Runcorn kicked dead, and at the interval Runcorn were leading by five points to nil. —On resuming, Bennett ran finely through the Runcorn team, and a score looked certain, but Moran intercepted his pass to Roscoe, and ran the ball out of danger, and from the next scrummage on the Leigh quarter-line, Jolley dropped a goal. Runcorn adopted defensive tactics after this. Result :—Runcorn, 2 goals I try (7 points), Leigh, nil. BARROW v. ST. HELENS.—THE SAINTS EASILY

An Interesting Record. A. native of Frodsbam, in the person of Mrs. Mary Annliearsley, completed her ninety-ninth year on Monday. For nearlj fifty yaars Mrs. Kearsley has resided at No. la,Cornbrook-road,Chester-road,Hulme. Her faculties are remarkably keen and her memory, though rather vague on the subject of dates, is clear and distinct enough in every other respect. She has had 13 children, of whom only two sons are now alive. She can very well rememlser the running of the first train between Manchester and Liverpool. Mrs. Kearsley lives quite alone in her one room, which is both bedroom and sittingroorn. The neighbours are very kind and attentive to her. Her companions are a cat and a brown linnet, of which she appears to be very fond. Though on the verge of a hundred, she can still read and sew. She has quite a pile of books on a side table which she has read over and over again. In literature her tastes are wide, and she says that she is quite as fond to-day of reading of battle love and murder as in her more youthful days. In warm weather she loves to sit with a book at the top of her cellar steps. Mrs. Kearsley can relate many interesting stories of by-gone Manchester.

EXTRAORDINARY SHOOTING TRAGEDY.

Fearnhead Platelayers' Sad End. I

An exceptionally pathetic suicide, committed in an extraordinary manner was investigated by Coroner Brig- house and a jury at the Farmers' Arms Hotel, Fearnhead, on Tuesday evening when an inquest was held on George Lunt, aged 47 years, a platelayer residing in Victoria- road, Fearnhead, who was found shot through the heart ou Saturday morning. Elizabeth Lunt, deceased's wife, stated that deceased had suffered from ill-health for a number of years, and had been to different doctors. During the last three ' weeks he had been unable to go to work owing to his suffering from nervous debility, and had been attended by Dr. Bennett, of Padgate. Dt .:eased appeared very , weak, in low spirits, and rather strange in his manner. On Friday last he told Irar that his club card, and other things of a similar nature, were in the cupboard. He added thnallithe coald not keep on looking after them, that he did not think he should be able to work any more, and that he was too weak. "Are you going to turn me out?" he asked. -he said : "No, we shall manage." She last saw him alive on the Saturday morning when she went to the market. She left her mother in charge of the house, and she found her husband dead on her return. In reply to the Corcner, witness said deceased left behind him a note which was in his handwriting, and he had evidently written it during the morning. The Coroner read the note as follows :—" Dear wife and children, forgive me for this rash act ; I cannot b3ar the pain any longer. Good-bye to all, and God bless you all, Your loving husband GEORGE." Elizabeth Dearden, aged eighty, mother of the deceased's wife, stated that when her daughter had gone to market, deceased came downstairs and after having had some cocoa he went for a walk. When he returned ten miautes later he mixed some brandy and water for himself, and went out again for ten minutes. He came back and went into the parlour as she went into the back yard. A few minutes later she heard a loud report in the house and on going into the parlour she found deceased lying on his right side on the floor and the gun was lying beside him. Vi hen she saw blood She gave an alarm. In answer to the Coroner, witness said deceased bad been ill, low and depressed ror some time. He was obliged to leave his work on account of continued ill- health.

The Coroner: None of you dreamt of him doing such a thing as this ? Witness : Oh, no.

Sergeant Mellor said that when he arrived at the house at 10.30 a.m. 3n Saturday he found deceased lying dead on the parlour floor in a pool of blood. A double- barrelled breech-loading gun was about two feet from deceased's feet. and it contained two spent cartridges. There was blood on the gun and also on a walking stick which lay close by. AMBULANCE COMPETITION AT WARRINGTON.

Contest for Crosfield Challenge Shield.

Ite value of first aid to the injured is being increas- ingly recognized in the country and, as recent disasters have shown, prompt assistance has proved an important factor in saving the lives of victims. Messrs. Crosfield and Sons have taken a foremost part in the forwarding of the ambulance movement, as not only have they an efficient corps connected with the works, but they have been warm supporters of the work in the North-Western District of the St. John Ambulance Brigade. Eight years ago they pre d a silver challenge shield for competition arno st the corps in the district and each year the contest has attracted wide attention. Fourteen teams attended at the Bank Quay works on Saturday to indulge in a trial of strength, and for the second year in succession Crompton emerged victorious. The examiners were Dr. E. Pim and Chief Superinten- dent of No. 4 district Garnett, and these two gentlemen gave the competing corps difficult questions to answer and intricate cases to deal with. On the whole the work of the men was excellent and an all-round intimate knowledge of first aid work was shown. The competing teams were Knutsford, Liverpool, Manchester, Oldham, Bolton, Crompton, Rochdale, Whitefleld, Burnley,Dukin- Hindley, Port Sunlight, Preston, and Radcliffe. The shield and the first prize of a carriage clock and case for each member of the team was won by Cromp- ton. Burnley were a good secorid and won handsome field glasses' while the third prize, silver photo frames, was won by Liverpool. At the close Captain Crosfield presented the prizes, in the presence of a large gathering. Captain CROSFIELD said it gave him great pleasure to I come and see their excellent drill and to distribute the prizes. Though he only saw the last two squads com- pete he noticed a marked difference in the smartness I with which the work was done as compared with two or 'three years ago—in short he could see from the appear- ance of the men on parade that there was far more discipline. The Crosfield Challenge Shield had now been competed for eight times and it had been won suc- cessively by Preston, Blackpool, Kendal (twice), Brier- field, Nelson and Crompton (twice.) It was very grati- fyinr, to his co-directors and himself that ambulance men took such an interest in competing for the trophy and they were glad that, in consequence of the enlarge- ment of the premises, it had been possible for the con- , test to take place at the works. Everyone who cared at all for unselfish labour must appreciate the good and patriotic work which ambulance men did. (Anplause.) Colonel TRIMBLE then took the opporttnity of per- sonally thanking Captain Crosfield for his presence. He also expressed the indebtedness of the Ambulance Brigade to Messrs. Crosfield for their great kindness and-liberality in presenting the shield, and said it would no doubt afford the firm satisfaction to see how their action had stimulated interest in the movement. Captain Crosfield said rightly and justly that since the time he first occupied the position of one of his (Col. Trimble's) staff officers he had noticed a great improve- ment in the Brigade. It was their aim and object to go on steadily improving. They were a body of very voluntary workers, and amongst such workers it was a difficult task to keep up discipline, but he thought they had succeeded. (Hear' hear.) The competition was the best numerically which had ever taken place for the shield. (Applause). They all appreciated the addi- tional encouragement extended by the firm in placing that fine hall at their disposal. Ambulance workers were doing considerably more than rendering first aid to the injured. Some of their members pelonged to the Royal Naval Sick Work Auxiliary, and had promised to serve their country if ever occasion arose. (Applause.)—Col. Trimble ooncluded by moving a vote of thanks to Captrin Crosfield and the firm. Chief Superintendent GARNETT seconded and pointed out that that was the first occasion on which the whole competition bad been decided on one day, and the success certainly justified the experiment. (Applause., The vote was heartily accorded and Captain Crostield responded. He briefly expressed the sense of the firm's indebtedness to Colonel Temple. Chief Suparintendent Garnett, and all the officers of the district for the great interest and the amount of trouble they had devoted to the competition. He hoped ambulence men thoroughly appreciated the self-sacrificing labours of their officers. (Applause.) The officers and teams afterwards sat down to tea and a very pleasant gathering concluded one of the most successful events in the history of the District.

THROWN UNDER A TRAIN.

_ ‘A terrible accident betel a young man named George Pheysey, aged twenty-four, of 3, Robert-street, Widnes, late on Friday night. He was seeing two companions off at the railway station, and after shaking. hands with them he jumped on to the footboard. In jumping off he misjudged the length of the platform and landed on to a coal-tip, which threw him under the train. So severe were his injuries that amputation of both arms was considered necessary, and the man now lies in Widnes Hospital.

It is an old saying that THE DURATION statistics can be mad 3 to OF OUR COAL pr ov e anything. Mr. SUPPLIES. Cinozze-MoNsr, Mr., is just now making use of statistict to prove that we are getting almost perilously near to the exhaustion of our coal supplies. It is an old story. In reply to a similar demonstration in regard to iron,a brother Member of Parliament, Mr. ARNOLD LuProN, who is more than a statistical authority,contends; with at least apparent success, that there is in this country enough iron are to last us for 1,000 years. It is a good number of years since Professor STANLEY JEVONS startled the country with his calcu- lations as to the probable duration of our coal supplies. It is a long time, indeed since he himself, in Carlyle's fine and solemn phrase "wended on into eternity." If the basis of his calculations had been sound our coal would now have become exhausted, or nearly so, and we should be a nation with decaying industries and diminishing population. But Professor Jevons lived long enough to discover that he had made a mistake. Mr. CHIOZZA-MONEY administers the necessary sedative, for his own alarmism when he quotes JEVONS as one of his authorities.fie forgets to quote Professor HULL, who read a paper on the national coal supplies at the meeting of the British Association something like twenty years ago, and who greatly extended the then prevalent view as to the probable duration of our stock of an indispensable fuel. Mr. CuiozzA-MONEY contends, with perfeet justice, that pre-eminence in modern industry goes with coal. Without coal, indeed, our main indus- tries, agriculture excepted, would perish. There would be an end of our iron and steel manufac- ture, of our ship-building, of our engineering. The prosperity of this country is, under existing conditions, based entirely on coal, and we should be justified in predicting a national collapse if our coal supplies were in any immediate danger. But we will point out to Mr. CHIOZZA-MONEY that two things have been going on concurrently for many years past—we have been taking ever-increasing quantities of coal out of the earth, and we have been receiving continual new proof of the' previously unanticipated wealth of the bank on which we are privileged to draw. Even Mr. CniozzA-IVIoNsv places the duration of the North of England coal•tield at something between 100 and 200 years. Fifty years ago, the late Lord ARMSTRONG, we believe, put it at about ninety. JEVONS gave it thirty years or so. The bases of calculation at the same time justified both JsvoNs and ARMSTRONG; but things have since become known that were not known is those days. Northumberland, which should now, if former vaticinations had proved true, be almost whollY given over to agriculture, is producing more coal than ever, is working larger and deeper collieries, and hadstores of coal the full extent of which is far from being fully knownin any case,it is difficult to perceiv a the utility of alarmism on such a subjectd. Were we to diminish our coal consumption an exportation because of an apprehended exhaustion of our supples we should have to slacken down our

ries to a quite ruinous extent. We shotV,l lace by slow de;rees the result that would oise occur tout it clan, as the French say. difference would he that which exists between ition and sudden death. e admit, however, that Mr. CinozzrggY's . for writing in a i•ather alarmist rnannqr on subject is a quite legitimate one, and an )11ent one to boot. He points out that Mr. -2oxn, convinced by the arguments of rofessor ST VNTLEY JEVINS, took immediate steps for reducing the National Debt, feeling that a which wag reapinc,, a crop which had harvest lt no seed time had no right to place a further on on posterity. It had no right, that is

say, to deprive posterity. of its coal supplies Ind saddle it at the same time with a vast trden of indebtedness. Mr. CiLADSTONE'S loninc.," was excellent, and his 'action much be an pl a nded. His reduction of the mal Debt, whilst he also reduced the number amount of the taxes, was one of the great Ternents of his lifetime. Unfortunately, he

followed by a prodigal Government. In the clarse of a few years the National Debt had been to nearly its former dimensions, whilst " amonnt raised from taxation has increased by I°lc)re than one-third, If Mr. CEITOZZA-MONEY'S 4,larolist articles should have the effect of convinc- -14 future Chancellors of the Exchequer that one their first duties is the reduction of the National e 4, which means not only of future but of Present burdens, he will establish a firm title to Pal)fie gratitude. FEEDING MR. ATKINS.

might well be daunted in his endeavour to reform the Army by the discovery of the multiplicity of the directions in which his zeal is called for. Not only must he find the Army and provide it with its brains, but—and he would satisfy Mr. Atkins at least—he must attend to its stornach,and see that it gets its food properly. His predecessor did something in that direction. He realised that the soldier, like most other human beings, can enjoy his food better when it is placed before him on a clean table-cloth with the accompaniments of civilisation than when he has to fetch it in a basin to the room in which he and a score of com- rades sleep, and eat it off the table on which boots and accoutrements have been cleaned. The "restaurant plan," which was first started by a battalion at Aldershot, was encouraged by Mr. A.rnold-Foster, and has proved a success not only in giving a semblance of decency to barrack-room feeding but in giving "single men in barracks" a much-needed variation in their daily fare. But the War Office never does things by• wholes, and. as a writer in the "United Service Magazine" points out, the "restaurant plan "is still incom- plete inasmuch as the cooks who prepare the food are untrained. Mr. Atkins does not require French dishes, but even plain cookinz,especially for a large body of men, demands previous knowledge and some skill. Yet in each battalion there is but one trained cook, and the actual work is often done by men who are detailed for the duty haphazard. Then, again, the War Office persistently disregards the advances which have been made in the con- struction of "cookers" on the obsolete stoves of thirty or forty years ago. Add to this the fact that meat inspection is a farce performed by sub- alterns who probably do not know beef from horse- flesh, and that preserved rations are often foisted on the men on various pretexts, and you have good proof that Pall Mall scorns the axiom that the way to a man's heart is through his stomach.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

The " Christian " name of one of the voters whose case came up at Southport Revision Court was "Judas Iscariot." " I wash my bands of him," said the Revising Barrister.

* * Are the " Springboks" going to repeat the triumphal march of the Fernlanders ? The question is agitating Rugby Unionists, for on Thursday the South Africans won their opening match against the East Midlands by 37 points to nil.

* * * Mr. Keir Hardie has not minced his words in informing the Liberal party that it is the intention of Socialism to smash, destroy, and pulverise Liberalism. Now we know where we are. Socialism was given the breath of Parliamentary life by the Liberals. It is the story of Frankenstein over :wain. The monster wants to destroy its creator.—" Referee."

* * * Why Mr. S. G-ough should try to get put on the voters' list for Great Sankey when he had been dis- qualified by the same barrister in Warrington, is "one of those things no feller can understand." Mr.Gough asked for "justice," and the barrister replied that he had got justice—too much justice. Time and trouble must be commodities which the irrepressible Samuel thinks nothing of wasting. * * ! You must find that impediment in your speech rather inconvenient at times, Mr. -Biggs ?" "0, n-no. Everybody has his peculiarity. Stammering is m-m- -mine. What is yours ?" " Well, really, I am not !aware that I have any." "Do-do you stir y-your tea with your right hand ?" " Why, yes, of course." " W-well, that is y-your p-peculiarity. Most p-people use a t-teaspoon." * * * " Bad as the slums are in Warrington,they are far better than those in Liver pool " is the opinion of Mr. Massey, the Trades Council delegate to a recent housing conference in the city by the Mersey. While this is undoubtedly the case, Warrington has plenty of work to accomplish before it can pride itself on the condition of some of the quarters in which workers live.

* * * " Warrin7ton Ratepayer's " remark in the course of his letter on the requisiton to the Mayor that " War- rindon has before now displayed a very strong objection to being rnlei by an autocrat " makes one wonder if ke speaks from bitter personal experience. At any rate, he appears to have a close acquaintance with that effective municipal moclus operandi known as the pulling of strings. * *.*

The special Council meeting on Wednesday hung fire for a moment or two, when the Mayor declared the proceedings open. Once it started, however, the 1 discussion was fast and furious. Almost the first item of note was the revelation that the much talked of counsel's opinions were in Liverpool at the office of the Corporation's agents. This seemed a serious mis- fortune at first, but perhaps it was a blessing in dis- guise. There has been quite enciugh " Counsel's opinion " revealed. * * Soon after commencing business on Wednesday the Council resolved itself into Committee in order that everyone could talk as often as he liked. Nearly everyone did, with the result that Alderman Bennett protested against the hubbub in the following terse, expressive sentence "This Corporation is more like a hennen than anything else; there is a continuous cackle all the time." This was about the only valu- able opinion the Alderman delivered himself of during the evening. He was not seen at his best! * * * One day recently a woman rushed into a chemist's shop carrying a little boy of three who had succeeded in cattin:,- off the top of his index finger by inserting it in one of those patent cigar-cutters that work with a spring. The wound was dressed and the sorrowing pair went home. Two hours later in came the same woman. This time it was she herself who was minus the top of her finger. She had been explaining to her husband how the hoy ant hurt. If he had any gallantry and finer feelings in him the husband ought to have gone back for the bits.

* * * Mr. F. Maddison, M.P. is regarded as an old friend by the Progressive workers of Warrington and there was a strong muster to hear his stirring address on Monday. He is one of those Knights of Labour who command respect by the perseverance which has raised them from the lowest rung of the ladder to our National Legislative Assembly, and he is a peculialy forceful and convincing speaker. The Examiner, Saturday, September 29, 1906. EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON

worn by the ensign on the heights of Alma, who was killed while marching to the attack, and whose life- blood still stains the buff. General Pileher made a stirring speech and all ranks were granted a holiday.

0 Mr. Samuel Brighouse the county coroner, generally shows good feeling and taste when conducting an inquest. He spares as much as possible the feelings of the relatives of the deceased on whom the inquest is being held and refrains from asking unnecessary questions. He is very rapid over his work, and it is evident he needs to be, if he has as many inquests as he had last Tuesday—for the case he had at Fearn- head, was the seventh that day —which necessitated a great deal of travelling up and down South-West Lancashire. Very few men could go through so much in a day, but Mr. Brighouse is an exceptionally active and strong man. 3 n Martyrdom is made easy at Widnes. Last week- end Mr. Daniel Garghan, one of the members of the Widnes Labour party, succeeded in his intention to go to prison for the right of "free speech." He had been fined by the magistrates and, of course, refused to pay. On Monday he applied to be committed to prison in default, but the magistrates were unable to oblige him forthwith. On Thursday, however, the necessary formalities were completed, and Mr. Garghan persuaded Inspector Strickland to receive him in charge. He has since been conveyed to Walton Gaol " in the highest spirits " to undergo imprisonment for 14 days. As a modern martyr to conscience Mr Garghan runs the suffragettes very close !

NATIONAL FREE CHURCH COUNCIL. Meeting of General Committee.

A largely attended meeting of the General Committee of the National Council of the Evangelical Free Churches was held on Monday at the Memorial Hall, London. The President of the Council Rev. J. Scott Lidgett M.A., occupied the chair and the following were among those present :—Mr. Robert Bird, Dr. David Brook, Mr. W. Brimelow, Mr. W. H. Brown, Dr. John Clifford, Rev. George Ears, Mr. T. H. Fleming, Rev. J. G. Greenhough, M.A., Rev. A. T. Guttery, Rev. Silas K. Hocking, Rev. J. H. Hollowell, Rev.C.Silvester Horne, M.A., Dr. R. F. Horton, Rev. S. B. Lane, Rev. Thomas Law, Mr. W. B. Luke, Mr. H. R. Mansfield, M.P., Mr. John Massie, M.P., Alderman Hugh Morton, Dr. G. Campbell Morgan, Rev. J.NI.G. Owen, Rev. James Pickett, Rev. J. E. Rattenbury,Mr. J. Compton Rickett, M.P., Rev. G. A. Johnstone Ross, M.A. Rev. J. H. Shakespeare, IVI.A, Sir John B. Slack, Dr. W. J. Townsend, Mr. Robert Whyte. The committee considered the education position and tho resolutions passed a few days ago by the Education committee were adopted. It was decided to hold a special meeting of the General Committee on October 30th to consider the desirability of summoning the whole of the National Council to review the situation and take united action in relation to the question of Education. If this be done all the delegates to the Annual Council held in 'Birmingham last March will be invited to attend. A very strong resolution against the traffic in opium and its disastrous results was unanimously passed. Rev. Thomas Low (General Seceetary) reported the new phase of the National Council work in the direction of the Harrogate Convention for the deepening of spiritual life. The Committhe agreed that this should be a permanent feature in the work of the National Council, and that in addition to Harrogate other centres should be visited next year. Probably Oxford will be one of these centres. It was decided to forward a petition to the Prime Minister asking him to afford facilities to discuss the Spurious Sports Bill. The General Secretary reported that the arrangements for holding the next Annual Conncil at Leeds in March, 1907, are in a forward state. All the local arrange- ments are well in hand.

BAND CONTEST AT RUNCORN. A band contest took place on Saturday at Runcorn under the auspices of the Cycle Parade Committee for the raising of funds for the Runcorn Cottage Hospital. The competing bands were the Riincorn Volunteer (conductor, Sergeant F. Walker), St. Edward's (Mr. Monaghan),Frodsham Volunteer(Sergeant Tom Knight) and Runcorn Amateurs (Mr. T. E.Lewis). They played from headquarters to the place of assembly, Devon- shire-square, where an initial contest for two prizes took place. Each played a selected march piece. The judge was Mr. J. A. Greenwood, of Egremont, and formerly of Runcorn. He awarded the first prize to Runcorn Amateurs, and the second (presented by Messrs. Unsworth Bros.), to Runcorn Volunteers. The bands then proceeded to Mr. Ackerley's plantation grounds, where the premier contest took place. The test piece was " Songs from Balfe." The first prize, a silver cup, value £lO, presented by Messrs. S. Taylor, F. J. Norman and R. H. Posnett, was awarded to the Runcorn Volunteer Band. Having been won by the band two years in succession the cup became their property. The second prize wa; awarded to St. Edward's Band, and the third to Frod- sham Volunteers. A special prize, a gold centre medll, presented ,by Messrs. J. Harris and Sons, to the best individual player, was won by Mr. T. E. Lewis. The cup and prizes were presented by Mrs. Taylor, who was thanked, on the motion of Mr. Lloyd, seconded by Mr. G. A. Kirkham. The performances were well attended. The trick cyclist, " Jack Sharp," gave several clever exhibitions in Devonshire-square and in other parts of the town, where collections were made for the fund. The Volunteer Band paraded the town with the cup during the evening. The arrangements for the contest , wet° carried out by Mr. G. A. Kirkam, hon. secretary I of the Cycle Parade Committee.
