Important Arrangements with the Rev. F. B. Meyer. _

It is well kown that the Rev. F.B. Meyer, 8.A., has resigned his pastorate and will close his ministry at Christ Church, Westminster Bridge-road, London, in the early spring of next year. For a number of years the I Rev. Thomas Law has endeavoured to secure the whole of Mr. Meyer'r services for the National Council. At the meeting of the General Committee of the Council held on Monday at the Memorial Hall, London, Mr. , Low stated that Mr. kleyet waS now prepared to devote I practically the whole of his time to conduct missions and meetings on behalf of the National Council. A resolution expressing the satisfaction of the Committee at this arrangement, was moved by Mr. J. Compton Rickett,M.P., seconded by Dr. R. F. Horton and carried unanimously. All the arrangements for Mr. Meyer's services will be made at headquhrters of the Council's Memorial Hall, and will all be under the auspices of the local Councils and Federations. Mr. Meyer will begin this special work in September 1907. Each mission will begin on a Saturday and include the Sunday and three or four following days. Mr. Meyer will reserve his Fridays for Committee and other work in London. Mr. Law states that after six or seven months of work in England Mr. Meyer will go to Africa for a few ' months and on his return will go on for another twelve months and then visis Australia. These visits abroad ' will also be under the National Council.

===== 0002601_19060929_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

FREE SPEECH AT WIDNES. SALVATION ARMY TO BE SUMMONED. On Saturday evening another stage was reached in the" Free speech "struggle at Widnes. The Salvation Army took up their stand shortly after seven o'clock, in Victoria-square, under the historic big lamp. After service had been going on for some time Detective- Sergeant Davidson appeared and, using the customary formula regarding " obstruction " asked the " officers to move on. They refused to go away, and the names of " Ensign " Martin, "Lieutenant ' Layelle, and "Private " Cawseywere taken. The two first-named , are lady officers. Ensign " Martin, addressing the crowd afterwards, said that when precious souls were in balance the officers of the Salvation Army were pre- pared to suffer imprisonment rather than give up their meetings.—Amengst those in the crowd was Councillor D. Lewis, president of the Free Church Council. He was observed to "pace " the distance from the edge of , the crowd to the footpath during the evening.—lt is understood that a member of the Army's firm of London solicitors were present watching the proceedings.

RUNCORN PETIT SESSIONS. At these sessions on Monday, before Messrs. J. ;Pritchard and E. Imison, John Hagan,Lightfoot-square, was fined 10s. for having been drunk and disorderly in Bridge-street on the night of the 15th inst. P.C. Holland proved the case. Herbert Fowler and Charles ; Bentham, young men, were summoned for having com- mitted a breach of the peace by fighting in Bridge-street lon the 15th inst. They both pleaded that they acted in , self-defence. P.C. Holland said he saw them fighting ' near Delph Bridge. Bentham had his jacket off. Bentham said Fowler and a man named Sephton were quarrelling. He asked Sephton to go away. Fowler thereupon struck him and he returned the blow. Fowler I had nothing to say. Defendants were ordered to pay Ss, 6d. costs each, and were bound over to keep the peace for six months.—Francis Millington,of Warrington was fined 40s. and costs for disorderly conduct Saturday I night.—Dennis Bryne, no fixed abode, was charged with I having wandered abroad and failed to give a satisfac- tory account of himself.—P.C. Wynne said he found him loitering about High-street at two o'clock in the !morning. He said he had no money and nowhere to go. I —Sergeant Bratt said he had been about the streets three nights in succession. He spoke to him several times. One night he said he had had no food for three days and witness gave him some supper, but told him that he would not be allowed to wander about the town at night.—The Clerk (Mr. Johnson) said the prisoner , had been examined by the prison doctor as to his sanity and the report was that there was nothing wrong with Ihim. There were several previous convictions, and prisoner was committed to gaol for three months.

WARRINGTON.

Coming Events. The annual concert under the auspices of the Warrington Apollo Prize Male Choir will be held in the Parr Hall on Monday, when an excellent enter- tainment is promised. An admirable programme has been arranged and will include contributions by Madame Rathbone, who has earned a high reputation in connection with the Scottish and Yorkshire choral concerts, Miss Worslev contralto, Mr. George Platt baritone, and Mr. Lindon Wyatt, humorist. The services have also been secured of Mr. John Paley who as a cornet player has few equals. The choir, under the conductorship of Mr. Harry Berry, will also con- tribute to the evening's enjoyment. The members of the Warrington Swimming Club bold their annual gala on Tuesday in the Legh-street baths and have arranged an attractive programme. The events to be decided include the Liverpool and District ladies' championship, and the works squadron, Warrington, and schoolboys' champion- ships. The proceedings will commence at 7.30. Particulars as to prices, &c. vill be found in our advertising columns. On Monday evening an organ and vocal recital will be given in Wycliffe Church by Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus.l3ac , and Mr. James Coleman, Lichfield Cathedral. The proceeds will be in aid of the renovation fund. The second of the series of University. Fxtension Lectures on "Robert Browning" will be given in the Co-onerative Hall, Sankey-street, on Wednesday evening. The Junior Liberal Association will commence its winter session on Monday evening when an open meet- ing will be held et the King's Cafe, Bridge-street. Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M,P., will preside.

In May of this year two lads, John Williams and Sohn Cloonan, Portland-street, observed two men cruelly illtreating a pony, and despite threats pluckily followed them and informed the police of the incident. Their evidence was alsb instrumental in securing the conviction of the two men. The Warrington Chess Club which was formed last year has just commenced its second season and intending members are invited to the rooms of the club at the Old Academy, Bridge Foot. The club meets every Thursday evening from 7.30 to 11 o'clock. Matches are being arranged With surrounding clubs and a tournament is to be inaugurated, the winner of which will bold the silver trophy for a year.—The secretary, Mr. J. R. Grammar School Swimming Championship. The annual race for the swimming championship of the Grammar School took place at the Corporation Baths on Wednesday afternoon. It Was somewhat disappointing that only three students .entered for the event—H. Adams. H. Turner, and —Collins—but considerable interest was taken in the race. Adams led throughout and won the championship belt for the third year in succession —a feat upon which he is to be congratulated. He covered the 150 yards in the Irecord time of 2mins. 4 1-sth secs., and was four yards in advance of Turner. Mr. Barlow acted as time- keeper. BABY CARRTAGES. Latest Datterns.—J.S. , GARLAND, 51, Bridge-street, Warrington.—Advt. Deputations at Warrington. The Housing Committee of the Dewsbury and District Infirmary, having been impressed with the economical management and excellent financial arrangement of the Warrington Infirmary,on Thursday a deputation visited the local institution. The party consisted of the chairman, eight members or the committee and the secretary. and was met at Warrington by Messrs. John Fairclough, Charles White, the senior house surgeon,and the Secretary by whom the local methods were fully explained. The internal arrangements of the Infirmary were also inspected and the deputation were very favourably impressed with what they saw and heard. Afterwards the party on the invitation of Mr. F. Monks visited the Warrington Nurses' Home and later inspected the "works of Messrs. J. Crosfield and Sons.—On Tuesday a large deputation from the Haslingden Board of Guardians visited Warrington for the purpose of inspecting the Warrington Workhouse Infirmary,which is considered a model of what such institutions should be.

Local Girl Swimmer's Success. At Birkenhead swimming gala on Thursday night Miss Muriel Barlow (daughter of the Warrington Baths Superintendent) won the Ist prize, an oak case of silver-plated knives and forks (12 pieces) in a 50yds. Ladies Open Handicap. There were 27 competitors. Schools Football League. The Warrington and District Elementary Schools Football League is now in full swing and weekly matches will be played to the end of the season. There are ten clubs in the League and the first matches were played last Saturday, the results being as follows : British 5, St. Mary's 3 ; Silver-street 2, St. Alba,n's 1 ; St. James' 4, Parochial 3 ; Heathside 1, Stockton Heath 1 ; St. Barnabas' 3, Hamilton-street 2. The matches for this week are: Wycliffe v. St. Barnabas ; St. Mary's v. Parochial ; St. -James' Y. British ; St. Alban's v. Heathside ; Stockton Heath v. Silver-street. The officials of the League are Mr. G. W. A. Brown, (chairman),Mr. I. Bowen (secretary); and Mr. Malcolm (treasurer.)

Lecture on Cromwell. There was a good attendance of members and friends at the Old Academy on Thursday evening on the occasion of a lecture on "Cromwell in Lancashire" which was delivered by Mr. Shelley, Liverpool. Mr. Lofthouse presided.—Mr. Shelley referred to the Scottish Raid under the Duke of Hamilton, the Battle of Preston, and the pursuit by Cromwell of the Royalist forces through Wigan to Warrington Bridge, where a battle took place. He also referred to Crom- well's lodging-house in Church-street and to the fact that the Great Protector visited Hill Cliffe Baptist Church where he listened to a sermon delivered by one of his officers.—The subsequent discussion was taken part in by Alderman Bennett, Messrs. J. Moore Murray, F. G. Taylor, C. Madeley and Dr. Naden. Musical Society's Next Opera. Interest in the Warrington Musical Society's fourth ' amateur operatic enterprise increases as the week of ' the production draws nearer. " lolanthe "—one of the most popular of the Gilbert-Sullivan collaborations —is to be performed at the Harr Hall for six nights commencing on Monday, October 15th, and there is every indication of a repetition of previous successes. The cast is a strong one, and while the services of many who have already achieved triumphs in opera have been retained, the public will be additionally interested in the appearance of several new-comers. The staging will be elaborate and full advantage will be taken of the opportunities which the play presents for the introduction of pleasing spectacular effects. We would remind our readers that the Infirmary extension fund will benefit by the production as the net procenls of Monday's and Friday's performances will be devoted to that deserving object. The plan 'of reserved seats will be opened on Wednesday next at 5.30 am. in the crush room at the Parr Hall. After 12 noon the bookings will be transferred to Messrs. Pearson and Payton, Limited, Bridge-street.

LAWN TENNIS, CROQUET, and other outdoor sports.—J. S. GARLAND, 51,Bridge-st.—Advt. Good Templar's Funeral. There was a large attendance at the funeral which took place at the Warrington Cemetery on Thursday afternoon of Miss Maggie Moody, of Liverpool-road, a prominent Good Templar and an auxiliary teacher at Sankey Bridges Wesle:yan Sunday school, who died on Saturday after a brief illness. Deceased held the office of Past Chief Templar of the Mission Lodge in Thewlis-street, and representatives of the South-West Lancashire District of Good Templars, as well as the members of the ladies' Bible class at the Sunday school, walked in the funeral procession. The last rites were impressively conducted by the Rev. W. Bracecamp and a special form of service used by the Good Templars was also gone through. In the even- ing -a memorial meeting was held in Thewlis-street schools when addresses were given by Bros. Morris and Dale and Sister Hardy.

Boxing Contest at Orford Barracks. The first of a series of competitions to be held at the Orford Barracks, Warrington, took place on Wed- nesday „night, and, judging from the enthusiasm which marked the evening proceedings, the competi- tions promise to be a success. The various bouts were keenly contested, and in two instances extra rounds had to be fought before the referee, Mr. T. Burns, of Widnes, could arrive at a decision. In the I.ost. to list, competition Private Tomlin, after a ' spirited contest,defeated Private Laurence. The final I for the recruits' featherweight ended in Private Hare I carrying off the honours, and the light-weight competi- tion ended in favour of Private Martin. Two rounds sufficed in the old soldiers' competition, Drummer Gleeson defeating Private Dillon. The arrangements I were made for the contests by Sergeant-Instructors Sweeney and Stamford. The prizes were distributed by the officer commanding the depot. Gift to Association Football.

The newly-elected President of the Warrington and District Football League, Hr. James Dodds, is dis- playing a great interest in the League, and has done much to stimulate the enthusiasm of the clubs by the presentation of a handsome challenge shield. The trophy is of a highly-Ornate design. The centre panel has (in repous4e work) a typical football scene, depicting a critical moment for the goalkeeper. Above this in oval shield is the Borough Arms tastefully engraved in correct detail. An ornamental scroll runs across the shield at the top, on which is inscribed " The Dodds Challenge Shield." In the lower panel the inscription reads :—" Presented by James Dodds, Esq., to the Warrington and District Association Football League -(First Divi- sion) for annual competition. September Ist, 1906." Surrounding the shield are 21 smaller shields on which will be inscribad the names of the various winners. IThe whole work is in massive solid silver with in-wrought design of oak and laurel wreathe in richly chased repous4e work and minuted on heavy oak support. The design is original and was specially prepared by Messrs. Eustance and Co., Ltd., silver- smiths, Sankey-street, Warrington, who have carried out the work in the highest style of the silversmith's art.

He said candidly that he could not conceive a better service to the working classes than a League with such objects as that, nor could heconceivea better organization for the member. because a man who was worth his gait liked to represent men who understood what politics were. It was the ignorant man who was always the most, unreasonable. He (Mr. Maddison) felt in quite a reminiscent humour that night. He thought of those excellent meetings when neither Mr. CrosSeld nor himself was a member of Parliament, and he would indulge in the luxury of reminding himself of what had happened in the meantime. They had lived through one of the most remarkable elections that the oldest man present had ever seen or, he believed, that the youngest man present would ever see. They had the whole country almost as one man RISING IN RKVOLT against a Prime Minister who played with politics as a mere game of the ins and outs, who was prepared to barter the industrial supremacy of England through her fiscal aysteni,in order to keep one party ia power. He (Mr. Maddison) said that no party was worth that, and if the temptation ever was succumbed to by their leaders and if the Liberal Government hung on to office month after month and year after year when they bad been obviously discredited by the country,, if they placed great national interests on one side in order to play their mere party game, they would deserve the same fate which overcame the Tory party at the last election., (Applause). It was an election indeed ! If every man. on the Opposition side—lrish Nationalists and Labour Representation group included—were to vote against the Government, and if every member were present, they would have 130 majority over the lot. (Applause). These figures threw a strong light on some of the silly nonsense one occasionally heard about what could be done against the Government. If the Irish Nationalists and Labour Representation Committee voted with the Government they would have the modest majority of 350. (Applause). That'was too big for any Radical. (Laughter). They were so used to being in small minorities that it was quite refreshing sometimes—and he had done it on more than one occasion this Session—to be in a small lobby where they had plenty of air and few people. (Laughter.) When one was with such an awful crowd, one began to think he had turned Tory. (Laughter.) The House of Commons was made up of a Irecord Liberal and Radical majority and they in War- rington played a splendid part in making up that majority. (Applause.) He could not help but con- gratulate the Labour League, Mr. Howes and all who took part in that splendid fight in a town which was, at any rate, not easy to win. He was not only glad that such a fine majority had been gained,but he was pleased to associate with their member. aleCrosfield. (Applause.) He had been brought into very close contact with Mr. Crosfield who was one of those modest,unassuming men who, because he (Mr. Maddison) happened to have been in Parliament three years before, often seemed to endow him with a supreme knowledge of the House of Commons. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

-- --- AN IMPUDENT THIEF.- At the Borough Police Court on Saturday, a respect- ably-dressed young man named Herbert Stanley Nelson of Bolton, was charged with stealing from the office of' the Wigan Coal and Iron Company, Ltd., Warrington, two hags containing £l4 175., the property of the com- pany. He admitted the offence.—The Chief Constable said that Mr. Robert Nelson, who was prisoner's uncle, was the foreman in the company's yard in Arpley-road. On September 15th he collected between .Cl 4 and £l5, and this he locked no in a safe in his office in the yard, locking also the inner and outer doors of the office. He concealed the office and safe keys in a hole in the plaster of the pantryceiling of his house,which was in the same yard—a very mysterious and remote receptacle that no one could hit on by guess work. He went out about nine o'clock, leaving the house unoccupied, and hiding the house key under an earthenware mug in the wash. house. This manner of disposing of the keys was well known to the prisoner and his brother, for up to six months ago prisoner worked with his uncle. On Sun- day morning Mr. Nelson missed the keys, and found them in the doors of the office and in the safe, the doors and safe being nillocked, and the money had gone. Detective-Sergeant Forster and Detective Clough had been working on the case since. It transpired that prisoner had been wandering about several Lancashire towns. On the Saturday he came to Warrington, then paid a hurried visit to Bolton as if he had not been to Warrington, and when he heard that the police were making inquiries into the case, came to the Police Station in most indignant style, and demanded to know why his name had been connected with the robbery. They assured him that he would get to know everything in good time, but every day he had called at the Police Station to see how the police had been getting on with their inquiries, in order that his name might be cleared. In this way he was evidently endeavouring to cast suspicion upon his brother. It had also been ascertained that since last Saturday prisoner had been releasing articles from pledge and paving several debts, including .£1 to his brother. On Friday his lodgings were searched, and between £lO and £ll were Sound between the bedding and mattress in his bedroom. He owned up at ones that he had com- mitted the theft. The Chief Constable added that since he lost his situation prisoner had lived on the generosity of his relatives.—Prisoner, who refused to say any- thing. was committed to prison for two months with hard labour.—The magistrates heartily complimented Detective-Sergeant Forster and Detective Clough upon the manner in which they bad unravelled the case.— The Chief Constable thanked the Bench on the officers' behalf, and and added that they had done very well. The Examiner, Saturday, September 29, 1906.

DID NOT WACT A NATION IN ARMS and he regretted extremely that Mr. Haldane ever used such an expression as that he wanted to popularise the conception of a nation In arms. That was carrying us back to the days of barbarism. A nation in arms belonged to the infant ages of the world and not to civilization. So far as Mr. Haldane meant that he was going to develop and give a chance to the Volunteers as against the Regulars they were with him. They wanted the Volun- teers not to be stifled and impeded and humbugged as ' they were during the last Tory Government. Obstacles wers placed in the way of the Volunteers by the Tory WIP,r Ministers and it was done for a purpose. That ptfrpose was to say that volunteering had failed and there was nothing left but conscription. The people would not have conscription. (Applause.) Radicals had their own independent views and they were not going to allow Ministers, be they Liberal or Tory, to try and popularise a conception which they believed to beinherently bad. (Applause.) Speaking for himself, he said deliberately that he did not want the conception of a nation in arms to succeed and he would do his very best to make it fail. So long as it was a mere question of giving fair play to the Volunteers to reduce the regular Army, then they would support Mr. Haldane right loyally and they had already in a small way thanked him for the reductions he had made, always telling him that it was only the beginning and they would want considerably more than they had got. Mr. Maddison went on to deal with TETE LAND QUESTION. _ _ . _

The great landlords, as John 'Stuart Mill said, grew rich in their sleep. While they were snoring in perfumed bedrooms, grimy miners were toiling, giving their life blood and sometimes their lives in order that the wealthy might reap the harvest of toil which was not theirs. Whatever the Labour League did, they must not forget Henry George. (Applause). lie (Mr. Maddison) did not proclaim himself a single taxer—he cbuld not absolutely see eye to eye in every detail with anybody, neither could his hearers if they thought. The great principle of Henry George, however,was that land was an uncreated thing, the gift of the great Father to the race as a whole, that land wasthe primary source of all wealth, the field of operation of all labour. There must be a land for humanity to live, and he who cornered land did in a modified degree the same as the man who cornered the air we breathed. Land was the basis of all reform. If they allowed land to be held for mere pleasure or profit as against the community, then whatever reforms they gained must fall short of fundamental. He (Mr. Maddison) did not believe in confiscation. "Is it because I am tender hearted towards the Duke of Sutherland ?" said Mr. Maddison amid some amusement. "Don't press me about that. (Laughter.) Is it that I would take the Countess of Warwick's laud? Would I take it by force? No. I might have the penalty of having to take her as well and that would be such a disadvantage that r ' prefer to let the land remain in the Warwick family." (Laughter.) He did not believe in confiscation, continued the speaker, not merely because it was immoral—that was enough—but because it was inexpedient. They could not confiscate without leaving the nation in a confiscating mood, and when they had tried their hand on the big landlord they would try their hand on the ordirwry worker if he happened to have a house and they had not. They could not stop in confiscation at that point or the other. He did not believe in confiscation in land or anything else but he believed they ought to

TAX LAND INTO USE. He had no sympathy with those who said they would tax land 20s. in the £ because they might as well take it at once if that were their view. He wanted to see us beginning to tax land into use, so that it would be more and more difficult to hold thousandsand thousands of acres just to use for the hunting season and to hold precious land in the middle of a great town until at last manufacturers and workmen went on their knees and were prepared to pay any price the landlord might ask because they must have the land or ruin their town. Their leaders had over and over again pledged them- selves to give effect to the taxation of land values and he was hopeful that they would make a statt in that reform. IVlr.Maddison pointed out how British industry was hampered by mining royalties which on pig iron amounted to 4s. 6d. per ton as against Bd. per ton in France and 6d. in Germany. The iron trade was handi- capped not by foreign competition but by

county have a fino side now that men like Lomas • Eagers, Little, Dixon and others have decided to assist the county of their birth. Lancashire tried twice to defeat the Cumbrians last season in Lanca- shire and failed to do more than draw each time. Anyhow there should be a good struggle. The County. I Championship is a more open event that it has ever been in the history of the Northern Union. This is all for the good of the game.

Warriagton " A " have a Combination game at Widerspool to-day. which should rejoice their hearts, for it gives promise of a keen contest for supremacy. The Rangers " A " took down Walker's Brewery last Saturday by 60 points to nil, so that Warrington owes them something. I think the " Champions." can just about put" paid "to the account. Lambert has not yet recovered from his injury, but the team is a good one : Knight, full back; Landor, Dickinson, Cart- wright and Harris, three-quarters; J. Davies and Jordan, half backs; Swift, Jolley, P. Boardman, Bradshaw, Forster, and Belton, forwards.

OUR -OWN STUPID LAWS:

Their Member was sound tt-Pc;i; those questions. As to how the reform should be achieved was another matter, and he (the speaker) warned them not to tie their Mem- ber's hands to details. They must keep him to prin- ciples and give him a warm time if he should depart from them. On the gloat principle of land reform they could have no better friend than Mr. Cresfield. (Applause.) Referring to work accomplished by the Government, Mr. Maddison said one measure—the Justices of the Peace Act—had received the Royal Assent. It removed the property qualification for county magistrates, and placed them upon the same footing as in the towns. The Government said that for a man to be on the Bench the test should not be found at his bankers, but in his moral, clean life, his clear brain, and his integrity. (Applause). An extremely good Irish Labourers' Bill had passed through the Com- mittee of the House of Commons. Mr. Redmond said that nothing that was English could be perfect in Ireland and he (Mr. Maddison) absolutely agreed with it. He hoped however that Mr. Redmond would be discreet in running that argument. The Merchant Shipping Bill—which conceded justice to seamen—had passed through Grand Committee. Mr. Maddison took occasion to protest against what he described as the un- English attack upon Mr. Havelock Wilson than whom no man in the House of Commons had done more for seamen. (Applause). With regard to the Workmen's Compensation bill, the trade union group and Labour Representation Committee group voted together in nearly every case on the amendments submitted. The

TRADES DISPUTRS BILE.

had also passed through comm-ii:tee7Re knew some would say that the Bill was not the ntrle measure that came into the House of Commons and why wasn't it? Was it because one section of men altered it? Not at all. The Bill introduced by Mr. Hudson was not drafted by either section of the House but by the Parliamentary Committee of the Trades Union Congress. He (Mr. Maddison) ballotted for the Bill as scores of other Liberal and Labour members did and it was only by the fortunae of the ballot that he (Mr. Maddison) had not to introduce the Bill instead of Mr. Hudson. Those members who sat on the Liberal side and who were trade unionists would, if there had not been a single tr Ade unionist on the other side of the House, have demanded from the Government and have got precisely what they had got in this Bill. The Government tempted to solve the diqlculty in their own way. It I was probable that the eifliculty would have been solved by the Government Bill but, in his oninion, in a chimes, way which would have been a temptation to trade unionists to be dishonest. What was wanted MS a straight Bill which would say that the funds of a trade union and of an employers' association should not be suable at law for that which occurred during a dispute. (Applause.) Mr. Maddison urged the workers to keep their own trade union instrument in their own hands and not to allow it be exploited by any political party. They only succeeded in altering the Trades Disputes Bill because the Liberal party by an over- whelming majJrity said " We pledged ourselves to something more practical than you have given us" and the Government could have carried the measure apart altogether from the mere Labour members. With I regard to the Plural Voting Bill the Government had I determined that only the man and not his wealth should I vote: He would not like to prophesy the fate of the Bill in the Lords. It would no doubt be in troubled waters. In the great movement for progress, he con- cluded,they had to remember that great reforms could only be carried if the people steadily knew what they wanted. They had got a Government in Power headed by the Premier—a man who had won the respect of the entire nation and the love of the House of Commons. Never did a Premier express the highest traditions of Liberalism and of the country more truly than when in the presence of the delegates of the Duma which was dissolved. Sir Henry cried " Long live the Donal." They were con- fident that the Government, backed up by a strong majority which included men who were at the head of some of the biggest Uaions in the country, would be able to do a great deal for the working people and the nation as a whole. Their object was to free industry, to free thought, to free everything in the country from the shackles of tyranny and the blight of monopoly. (Applause).

===== 0002601_19060929_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
THE HOUSE OF LORDS.

Mr. JOSEPH HOWES, in an interesting address, dealt with the question of the Elouse of Lords, pointing out that it was entirely separate from the ques- tion of a Second Chamber. If they abolished the House of Lords they could still have n Second Chamber. Mr. Howes traced the history of the House of Lords, stating that it came into exist- ence as the result of pride and prejudice and that always been the stronghold of pride and prejudice. One Peer in the House,of Lords had as much power as 11,000 electors. For a good many years the Lords had been deaf to the demands and blind to the needs of the nation's social life. The House could be abolished in a perfectly Constitutional manner, although it would take one or two elections. FREE EDUCATION.

In moving a vote of thanks to the speakers Mr. E. ngland endorsed Mr. Marldison's view that we did not want a nation in arms. fie urged the members of the League to carry Liberal principles into local legislation and said he considered that the technical school, which had been built and was maintained from the rates, should be free to the children of all classes.

===== 0002601_19060929_art0079.txt | included=true | included =====
LIBERAL LABOUR CO-OPERATION.

Mr. J. MORTON seconded. He said there was a section in the House of Commons who tried to make themselves believe that the Liberal party was as bad as the Con- servative party, but he was pleased to find indications of a change of view. Quite recently Mr. G. D. Kelly, a Labour member, said he never knew a Government which had attempted to do so much for the workers as the present Government. They were getting what Labour had been clamouring for years. Mr. Morton thought that eventually there would be a merging of extreme parties and a formation of one solid Labour party. The motion was heartily carried and a similar com- pliment to the chairman concluded the meeting.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

[By " Ovalist."]

Warrington have yet to meet their equals at the new game—possibly they will to-day —but on Satur- day Widnes went the way of Hull and St. Helens when opposed to the " wirepullers " and suffered defeat by exactly the same figures as last year, viz., 31-2. In the 42 games played between Warrinkrton and Widues in the last twenty years, the Widnesiansr have only won ten; Warrington having 28 victories and 4 draws to their credit.

The home team's first thirteen was changed on Saturday for the first time, owing to the absence of Isherwood and Fish. Still, even with such prominent absentees Warrington's superiority was most mani- fest, Dickenson and Harris depurtising on the left wing in creditable style. Widnes made a very promising start and for a quarter of an hour more than held their 'own. Sheer dash and determination kept them on the offensive and a penalty goal was only slight reward for their exertions. Nevertheless, it was an eye-opener for the home crowd to see the visitors showing so bold a front. The opening score roused Warrington to the conviction that something must be done and a vigorous attack was the result. Harris just failed to ga in after a passing bout, but from the succeeding scrummage Hockenhall stood wide for Lees' pass and dashed over near the posts. Preston added a goal, and confidence again reigned supreme in the Warrington camp. Fast sork by the backs kept the Widnes defence busy and before half- time Harris put the finishing tiuch to a brilliant series of transfers.

On resuming, Warrington's score was expected to be doubled, but as a matter of fact it was quadrupled before the close. A short lecture from Mr. T. Bowers had the desired effect, and we saw less punting and more half and threequarter combination in the second moiety. This is Warrington's strong point, and when the backs got fairly on the run the Widnesians were soon left in the rear. Goals and tries came from all directions and in the last eight minutes an equal number of points was obtained, bringing the total up to 31. Altogether Harris got two tries, and Hocken- hall, Brooks, Taylor, Boardman, and Harmer one each. Preston kicked four goals and Berry one.

Taken all round it was a very fair game. There was plenty of excitement for the spectators, and some good football at times. The one drawback was the amount of punting, and Warrington were much to blame in the first half for following the visitors' example. If, instead of returning so many of the kicks from Tilley, Lally and Co., the backs had gone in for passing, they would have made more headway and started scoring earlier. Widnes could kick as well as Warrington, but in the finer points of the game they were much inferior. lam always surprised when Warrington allow themselves to be led into a punting contest. They never get any -advantage from it, and the forwards are generally run to a standstill if it is kept on long enough. On Saturday they changed their tactics in time, or the margin would only have been a small one.

Chief praise must g 3 to Ike Taylor who, against his old club played a magnificent game in the centre. He was a perfect glutton for work and combined beautifully with Brooks. In fact this wing is little inferior to the more famous one of Isherwood and Fish. The latter pair will have to look to their laurels in the scoring line. Lees and Hockenhall were also first-class and work mach better together than formerly. Berry was a bit better than Tilley though the latter did some brilliant things under difficulties, while the forwards were well-matched. Widneswere wanting in weight and this told against ththetain he scrummages. By the way, the Warring- ton foi wards are beginning to pass like budding three-quarter. On Saturday, Boardman raced in from the quarter line after taking a pass in the neat- est fashion, while Harmer, Heath,Preston and Thomas are quite experts. Naylor is still a bit clumsy with his hands but he is a rare worker.

There is only one 100 per cent, in the league just now, and thit is possessed by Hunslet, who are play- ing like the champions they virtually were last season. Warrington are second in the race, with Halifax a close third. These are the only undefeated teams, but there may be changes this week-end. Hunslet go to Wigan —usually a burying ground for them—War- rington to Broughton, where the issue is open, and Halifax should be safe at home to Hall. But as they could not beat Batley on Saturday,and Hall are a team of surprises it would not do to predict; very confi- dently.

The New Police Court Missionary

On Monday morning at the Borough Police Court, the new Police Court Missionary, Mr. Firth, was introduced to the magistrates and officials by the Rev. Hirst, of Liverpool. The Mayor extended to Mr. Firth a very cordial welcome, and expressed the hope that his work would be a great success in Warrington. There was, he said, plenty of scope for his efforts in the town and district.

Garden Party. A garden party in aid of Dr. Barnardo's Homes, was -held in connection with the Young Helpers' League at Massey Hall,ThelwalLon Saturday afternoon. There was a good attendance of ladies and gentlemen, and a pleasant afternoon was spent in tennia, golf, croquet, and boating on the lake. An excellent tea was served in the open air, and was thoroughly enjoyed. Several selections of music were given at intervals, including whistling solos, songs violin, and 'cello duets, trios, and piano solos and duets, and were greatly appreciated by ;the party. The effort was in every way a great success.

BOOTS IN LANOASHIRt—AdVt. Anthrax at tiorley.---:Cheshire Farm Labourer's Death.

A curious case of anthrax has just come to light, and unfortunately has been attended by fatal results. About a fortnight ago a cow was killed on a faxm at Norley. It was apparently in good condition, and was dressed and despatched to the Manchester Meat Market. Here it was examined by a meat inspector, who found symptoms of anthrax. Eneegetic steps were at once taken to prevent infection. A number of carcasses which it was thought might have come in contact with the diseased meat were destroyed, as also• were a large number of hides. Some eight or nine days after the carcass had been sent from the farm at Norlev two of the farm 'lanai —John Henry Aston and William Gerard —became ill, and a doctor pro- nounced them suffering from anthrax.e They were conveyed at once to the Royal Infirmc.ry, Liverpool. Despite every attention, however, Aston's • condition rapidly became critical, and be died on Tuesday morning at seven o'clock. • Gerard is making good progress towards recovery.

UNDER THE CLOCK for Good BOOTS. Advt.— Appleton Rifle Club.

On Saturday afternoon last, the final monthly com- petitions of the season were held at the range, when the " Donegall " Bronze Badge —which is offered annually by the National Rifle Association—was won by Sergeant-Drummer J. W. Plinston. There was a good attendance, and some close shooting took place in several of the competitions. In spite of a rather poor light, several remarkably good scores were made. The handicap in the " Donegan " Bronze Badge com- petition vi as fixed on the results of five_ preliminary shots fired by 'each competitor, who subsequently each had ten shots at a stationary target (with 31 inch bull's-eye), and ten shots at a disappearing figure target, the distance being 100 yards, and the service rifle and full service ammunition being used. The highest possible score was 180 points. The following were the three best scores :—Sergeant-Drummer Plinston, 78 points ; Sergeant Robin, 48 points ;, Mr. Seger Owen, 45 points. The second prize was awarded to Sergeant Robin. The Members' Stakes consists of seven shots (in addition to a sight- ing shot) at a 6-inch bull's-eye target, at 100 yards, the highest possible score being 28. Any rifle may be used. This resulted in a tie between Sergeant-Drummer Plinston_ and Sergeant Robin with 27 mints each— the first and second prizes being pooled and divided between them. The conditions of the open sweepstakes are five shots, with any rifle, at a 31 inch bull's-eye target at 100 yards—the highest possible score being 25. The first prize was won by Lance-Sergeant Stewart, with a score of 20 points. Three competitors tied for the second prise, with ,scores of 18 each, but on a re-shoot, Sergeant-drummer Plinston proved himself the best mirksman. On the 15th of this month,Lance- Sergeant W. Stewart was again successful in winning the " Rifleman Minature Certificate," with a score of 116 out of a possible 150. This is the second of these certificates which: has been *on by him during the short portion of the season that he has been a member of the club. On the whole, the season has been more than usually successful, and several new members have been elected The competitions, week by week, for the "Rifleman Certificates" have proved • very popular, and there has been marked improve- ment in marksmanship. If the committee could feel assured of enrolling sufficient ,new members.to make such a course feasible, they would gladly consider the possibility of extending the usefulness of the club next year, by making a reduction in the amount of the Isubscription and by opening the range on another afternoon, in addition to Saturday as at present. ,

FLORRIE'S DREAM.

poor-widow with her only child once lived in the border of a forest. Morrie, as the child was called, was a pretty little girl, but she had. one great fault: she was idle, and cared neither to

go to school nor to help her mother at home. This was all the more unkind, as since her father's death, Florrie's mother had been obliged to' work very hard to earn bread for herself and child. One bright summer day the widow was called away from home, and before leaving she told Florrie to make things nice-and tidy before her return. Instead of dcring this, however, Florrie sat by the open door a long time after her mother left, gazing idly into the depths of the forest, and watching the butterflies and birds that darted to and fro among the trees. All at once she saw a patch of pale primroses growing by the side of a brook that ran through the wood. " Ah," thought Florrie, "any time will do for my work. • Mother cannot expect me to keep indoors this fine summer day." So off she ran and began to pick the primroses and other wild flowers, until quite tired out, she threw herself down to rest under the shade of a large pak tree, and there fell fast asleep Suddenly lifting her head, Florrie thought srie heard a strange noise, which seemed to come nearer and nearer, and, on springing to her feet, she was surprised to see all her work coming towards her. First came a bundle of sticks and the coal scuttle, then the kettle on four little legs, toddled after them, followed by the broom, pail, flannel, duster, and work-box. Lastly the stockings that her mother had told her to mend, came walking towards her, the great holes in the toes showing to advantage in the bright sunlight' As soon as this strange group came up to Florrie they began to make a great noise and clatter. "Light us quickly," cried sticks and coals, tumbling over each other. "Fill me from the spring," sang the kettle, at the top of its voice. "Mend us" said the stockings.; "sweep, wash, scrub, and dust with usl" screamed the broom, pail, flannel, and duster. Not content with shouting the broom began to beat Florrie so soundly that she started off home at full speed, thinking that the best thing that she could do was to clear up the place as quickly as possible, unless she wanted the broom to break itself across her back. Now Florrie could work very ,well when she chose, and soon began to work. Then she lighted the fire, got the hearth nicely swept, the floor scrubbed, and the supper laid neatly on the table, whilst the flowers that had given her so much trouble made the room look bright and cheerful. Having made things neat and tidy, Florrie washed and dressed herself, and then sat down‘to mend her stockings. She was wide awake now; but her dream bad taught her that they only are truly harl'Y who are wise enough to prefer duty tq pleasure.

I remain,

Your affectionate niece, NANCY E. PORTER.

With love to Uncle Ned, frcm WIN= ELLIOTT.

THE PRINCESS ON THE PEA.

There was once ai Prince who wanted l° marry a Princess; but she was to be a real Princess. So he travelled about, all thrGulo atl world, to find a real one; but everywhere thero was something in the way. There were Prin- cesses enough, but whether they were real Princesses he could not quite ma.ke out; there was always something that did not seem quite right. So he came home- again, and was quito sad, for he wished so much to have a real Princess.

One evening a terrible storm came on. lightened and thundered; the rain streamecl doWn; it was quite fearful! Then there wa:; a knocking at the town gate, and the old King went out to open it. It was a Princess who stood outside the gate. But, mercys how she looked, from the rain and the rough weather! The water ran down from her hair and her clothes; it ran in at the points of her sheee, and out at the heels; and yet she declared that she was a real Princess. "Yes, we will -soon fin.d that out," thought the old Queen. But she said netting, only went into the bed-chamber, took all the bedding off, and put a pea on the flooring of the bedstead. Then she took twenty mattresses, and laid them upon the pea, and then twenty eiderdown beds upon the mattresses. On this the Princess had to lie all night. In the morning she was asked how she had slept. ' ".oh, miserably! " said the Princess. "I scarcely closed my eyes all 'night long. Goodness knows what was in mY bed. I lay upon sonatthing hard, so that I ant black and blue all over. It is quite dreadful!" Now they saw that she was a real Princess, for through the twenty mattresses and the twenty eiderdown beds, she had felt the, pea. No one but a real Princess c-Julft be so delidat,'. •So the Prince took her for bis wife, for now he knew that he had a true Princess.

WINIFRED ELLIOTT.

UNCLE NED.

DEATH'S TIMEPIECE.

At the time my story begins I wae in Spain enjoying a well earned holiday from my London office. I was staying at the same hotel as a very beautiful young lady: This young lady was also from London, and had come with her father and mother. The latter was a very fragile woman. Every evening I played cards with the lady and her father, and became very friendly with both. One morning the servant brought me a letter which, to my horror, read as follows:

"You are ordered by the "Secret of Secrets League" to kill a young lady residing at your hotel. This night one of the League will meet you in your room, and will point out to you this lady. 'Remember, disobedience is ' Death.' "

Now, some years ago, when I was young and foolish, I joined a league who called themselves the "Secret of Secrets." This letter was an order from them to murder perhaps the young lady I played cards with. After I had read it I felt sick and ill, but I determined not to do it. Leaving the hotel I went to the police Inspector, explained matters privately and briefly, obtained three policemen and hid them in a cupboard in my room. About nine o'clock my man came and was captured after having passed the sign with me. After this I went out as I felt very faint, and as I was strolling along I was suddenly borne to the ground, and a dirty rag was stuffed in my mouth. I was caught by several members of the "Secret of Secrets." They blindfolded me, and carried me some dis- tance till they stopped at a door, which they opened. , Having thrown me inside they pro- ceeded to unbind me. Then one of them took the lead to a room upstairs. Having opened a door and pushed me in, the leader, who was a very powerfully-built fellow, said to me with El grin, "Now this is your room." I looked. On one side of the room was the dial of a Large clock about 10 feet high. In the middle of the dial of this clock and protruding from the centre of the hands was a steel point. In line with this point about six feet back was a curi- ous chair, with. straps all over it. There was a hollow for the head to fit in, and a steel cap to keep it in it place. Having shown me this they took me to a small place behind this enor- mous clock. Here were the works. Running right through the middle of them was a round bar, one end was the point I had seen in the dial, the other end fitted in a socket which was made to aclvance towards the works by a chain fitted to a wheel. Three inches above my head were several of these bare of different lengths. The leader took one of the longest down and put it in the 'clock in the place of the other, which he took out. Then he said, "You have betrayed one of us, and now you shall die in a manner you will not appreciate. We shall strap you in the chair which I first showed you-, and this bar will gradually advance toward you till the point goes through you." Seeing this was to be my fate I leapt upon the man, bringing down the bars above. One of these wounded the leader severely. I was then instantly dragged into the chair and strapped. 'FITZROY LOVELL. (To be continued.)

Dear Uncle Ned„—l have not yet seen a letter in your Columns from the Isle of Wight, awl I should like to write one about a very inftr- eating donkey there is on the island. Near Newport, the capital, are some ruins of an old castle called Carisbrook. Charles the First was imprisoned in this castle, and they used to draw their water from a well-house, 'which may still be seen; the well is about two hundred feet deep. In this house is a huge wheel that draws up the water. This wheel is moved by a donkey walking up and down inside of it, and keeping it continually in motion. And so for hundreds of years the ancestors of this donkey have been doing that work, which work seems to agree with them, as this one is twenty-two years old, and the last one lived to be nearly forty.o We are living in a very in- teresting old house here. It was the first house in Ventnor. It was once the manor house sbf Bonchurch, and is very old-lashioned.—l re- main, your reader, M. P. '

The Examiner, Saturday, September 29, 1906.

LIFE-LONG NEURALGIA CURED.

Grateful Mother's Testimony to

Bile Beans

Neuralgia, a very prevalent ailment at this time of the year, means nerve-starvation. Bile Beans cure neuralgia by setting right the functions of digestion and nutrition, and thus feeding and strengthening the sta*ved and weakened nerves. Mrs. Annie Elizabeth Pottage, of 51,Nornabell-street, Holderness-road, Hull, has told a "Hull Daily News " reporter lac:47 Bile Beans cured her of life-long suffer- ing from neuralgia. "The neuralgia brought on tooth- ache and sickness," she said. "1 used to be very sick for two days together. At night I couldn't sleep at all for the terrible pain, and during the daytime I was weary and listless. I went to he Infirmary and the Dispensary for treatment. I have had seven teeth drawn out at one time, but in spite of it all I got no better. _

_ . "A short time back I tried Bile Beans, and I soon noticed an improvement in my health. I continued takihg the Beans, and I was delighted to find the improvement continued. Now my health is much better than it has been for tears; I sleep well, and am healthy and strong. Since I took Bile Beans I have not had a touch of my old complaint." Bile Beaus, the world's most successful tonic-altera- tive, are sold only in sealed boxes, of all chemists, or post free from the Bile Bean Mfg. Co., 4, Red Cross Street, London, E.C., at Is. lid. and 28, 9d. (triple size).

PENKETH REVISION COURTS. Mr. S Cough's Vote Disallowed.

Mr. James T. Fpard, barrister-at-law, attended at the Conservative Club, Stocks-lane, Penketh, on Tuesday morning, to revise the lists of voters for the townships of Bold, Cuerdley, Great Sankey, and Penketh. The Conservatives were represented by Mr. J. H. Bottomley (organizing secretary of the division).The assistant over- seers present were :—Messrs. James Houghton (Bold), E. Forster (Penketh), Joseph Smith (Great Sankey),and James Wright (Cuerdley). The Conservatives objected to the name of Mr. S. Gough, of 106, Winwick-street, Warrington, being retained in the Parliamentary or parochial electors list of ownership electors for the township of Great Sankey in respect to a house in Chapel-lane. The Barrister: I have an objection to Mr. Gough which is rather fatal, I am afraid. (To Mr. Gough) : I cannot assist you. I would if I could. I can do nothing for you. • You know the objection is fatal. You have been guilty of bribery and reported, and that ought to be sufficient. Mr. Gough : May I be allowed to see the certificate? I may raise an objection to it. The . Barrister : You can see the paper. . . . - . . . . . .

Mr. Gough (looking at it) : I sutimit that this is not a certificate of the Court. It does not bear the stamp of the Court and is not, therefore, admissable. The Barrister: We take an accepted fact, a proved fact. It is sufficient that the conviction is common knowledge now. It is an established fact, and has been established in a court of justice. Mr. Bottornley : I put in the signed certificate of the Town Clerk of the County Borough of Warrington. Mr. Gough : I submit that it is not according to law, and not admissable in evidence. .

The Barrister : But you are not entitled to challenge it nor are they compelled to produce it. The fact that you have been before me places you at a disadvantage. I know you have been so convicted from certain facts produced before me, and the certificate signed by Sir Herbert Stephen. You are undoubtedly debarred by the statute and fora period longer than lat first sug- gestel. I thought the proceedings had been taken under a particular section that debarred you only for live years, but under section 6 it is for seven 'Fears. Mr. Gough: Cannot I get justice? I think that is the only thing I am asking for. The Barrister: You get justice, and too much justice. That is the objection you are labouring under. I should think seven years is a long time, but I cannot alter it. Mr. Justice Bigham was the person who deter- mined that. I can do nothing in the matter.

WARRINGTON. Mr. Foard also attended at the Warrington Police Station on Tuesday afternoon to revise the lists for Poulton, Rixton, Warrington and Woolston. Mr. J. H. Bottomley and Mr. W. A. Mawdsley represented the Conservatives, and the assistant overseers present were Mr.W.H. Woodcock(‘Va'rrington),Mr.G. Stubbs (Kixton- with-Glazebrook), Ur. 3. Daintith (Woolston-with- Martinscroft), and Mr. F. Wright (Padgate). The ownership claims and re-claims included 32 from War- rington, all of which were allowed, but the business was of a 'routine character and of no public interest.

===== 0002601_19060929_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
TRADES COUNCIL AND NOVEMBER ELECIIONS.

Councillor Wright and the L.R.C.

The most important business under consideration at a special meeting of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council, on Tuesday, was the question of their action at the coming November elections. At the previous meeting the Council, in response to an enquiry from the Labour Representation Committee, with which they are aililiated,expressed themselves in favour of contest- ing at the elections and nominated Councillor Wright as the candidate to stand in the Bewsey Ward in the labour interests. Councillor Wright had previously, been nominated to the Labour Representation Com- mittee by the Gas Workers Union, but had refused to accept the'nomiriation, and on Tuesday Councillor Stott announced that Councillor Wright had written to him decling the nomination of the Trades Council.

Councillor Wright wrote:—Onthe7th inst. I ! informed the Labour Representation Committee that I could not stand as a Labour Representation Committee candidate not only because I felt myself ineligible, but because the Labour Representation Committte at the ' present moment is not in a financial position to run an election. I would also say that the Labour Representa- tion Committee lacks the proper spirit and number of workers that is necessary to fight _an election with any hope 'of success, and therefor very respectfully decline the kind invitation of the Trades Council. I think that my two years work at the Town Council is worthy of a little more consideration than to allow mysef to be put forward with nothing but hopeless defeat staring me in the face through lack of spirit, finance and workers. I see in the Press a statement by your Chairman that I had been a member of my Trade Union -for ten or twelve years. That statement was contradicted by Mr. Massey. Will you in fairness to me and in the interests of truth state to your Council that my entrance into the Wiredrawers Society dates from December, 1895. Will Mr. Massey kindly withdraw and allow the Press to make the correction. Mr. HALEY pointed out the difficulty in which the Council were placed with regard to nominating another candidate to contest Bewsey Ward. If Councillor Wright accepted the nomination of any other organiza- tion and the Trades Council also ran a candidates it would make confusion. worse confounded.

Mr. PODMORE : If he refused to accept our nomina- tion he refuse the nomination of the gas workers, and will not come out under Labour Representation Committee colours.

Mr. MASSEY said he did not remember that he flatly contradicted the chairman's statement at the previous meeting. He expressed a doubt because a member of the Society told him Councillor Wright had only been a member eight or nine years. He accepted Councillor Wright's statement.

Th-e question of the future action of the Trades Council with regard to the elections was adjourned.

AN APPEAL.

An appeal for financial aid from the committee of the Havelock Wilson indemnity fund was allowed to lie on the table.

HOUSING OF WORKING CLASSES. Mr. MASSEY presented an exhaustive re port on the recent conference in Liverpool on the Homing question which he attended on behalf of the Trades Council. After the conference he visited some of the slums in that city and had come to the conclusion that some of the Warrington slums bad as they were, were better than the slums in Liverpool.

The report was adopted. _ SHOP ASSISTANTS AIL THE COMPENSATION BILL.

Mr. BRADRURN moved " That this meeting claims that all shop workers, whether employed in large or small establishments, should be included under the pro- visions of the Workmen's Compensation Act Amend- ment Bill and, in order that the benefits should not be illusory,asks the Government to insert a clause in the Bill setting up compulsory state insurance on the lines of the resolution adopted at the recent trade congress." The shop assistants were anxious that the Bill should pass its third reading in the House of Commons and that everything possible should be done to counteract the opposition received from Grocers' Federations throughout the county. Mr. PLINSTON seconded. The amendment proposed vas absolutely necessary if the Act was to accomplish the object for which it was intended. Mr. SOITER FIELD pointed out that to insure assistants would cost the employer ss. per cent. or id. per week on every £ paid in wagas. The resolution was unanimously carried.

THE FREE CHURCHES.

THE EDUCATION QUESTION.

We are now on the eve of the renewal of the struggle over Education, and all sides are look- ing eagerly forward to the re-assembling of Par- liament to see what action will be taken by the House of Lords. In some quarters it is freely prophesied that the Peers will deal most drastically with the Government's measure, whilst others are of opinion that, as is usual with our Upper Chamber, the measure will go through with protests. In any case, Free Churchmen are thoroughly on the alert and de- termined that the Lords shall not stand in the way of the will of the people. The National Free Church Council will be taking a strong lead. A special meeting of its Education Corn. mittee has just been held at the Memorial Hall. The Rev. J. Scott Lidgett, M.A., the President, occupied the Chair' and as showing the impor- tance and representative character of the gather- ing it may be mentioned that amongst those present were: Dr. John Clifford, Dr. J. Monro Gibson, Rev. A. T. Guttery, Rev. J. Hirst Hollo- well, Rev. C. Silvester Horne, M.A., Rev. R. F. Horton, M.A., D.D., Mr. A. E. Hutton, M.P., Rev. S. B. Lane, Rev. Thomas Law, Mr. H. R, Mansfield, M.P., Mr. R. W. Perks, M.P., Mr. E. C. Rawlings Sir John Bamford Slack, and Mr. Robert Whyte.

===== 0002601_19060929_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
WEST RIDING JUDGMENT.

This Education. Committee expressed its satisfaction that the judgment of the Court of Appeal in the case of the West Riding Edu- cation Authority vindicated the principle for which the National Council contended—that the Public Authority should not be held respon- sible for the maintenance of denominational instruction over which it had not, and could not have control. The Committee, however, re- corded its judgment that this decision did not in any way lessen the need for legislation to secure a completely national system pf education, and to abolish ecclesiastical and theological tests in the appointment of teachers to schools main- tained by the public. Clearly the Committee is not of the opinion that the West Riding Com- mittee renders unnecessary the Government's Bill. Such a view only takes into account a part of the grievances t against which Free Churchmen protested -when 'llr. Balfoin's Bill was introduced. With regard to the judgment itself, the, Committee suggest that the Local Councils should urge the Local Education Authorities to refuse to impose a rate for denominational teaching in State Schools, and that where the rate is levied deputations of ratepayers should challenge the legality of that portion of the rates before the auditors.

===== 0002601_19060929_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
FUTURE ACTION.

It is understood that the General Committee of the National Free Church Council will convene a special meeting directly the action of the House of Lords is made clear. At this meeting it will be considered whether the crisis makes it necessary to summon a special National Council to deal with the situation. It will be remembered that at the last National Council in Birmingham in March a resolution was passed authorising the Executive to call a special • sessions of the National Council if necessary. Should the House of Lords propose any drastic alterations there can be no, doubt that a fierce fight will ensue.

===== 0002601_19060929_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
PROPOSED GREAT DEMONSTRATIONS.

In order to make clear the Free Church attitude on education the National Free Church Council proposes to hold a series of demonstra- tions in the principal centres in the country. The first of these great gatherings will be held at Derby on October Bth, to be followed by others in the larger towns of the land.

===== 0002601_19060929_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
GIPSY SMITH AND AMERICA.

The National- Free Church Council have arranged for Gipsy Smith; their well-known Evangelist, to take an ,extensive tour in the United States, and he is now on his way there. A hearty " Send-off" was given,liim in a series of meetings at Liverpool on September 23rd and 24th. On the first of these days the Rev.. Thomas Law represented the National Council. An extensive itinerary has been arranged. October will be given to Greater New York. In November he will conduct united meetings at Tremont Templet Poston. December will. be spent in Maine, and the first two weeks iri January in Chicago. In February and March he will visit Peoria and Galesburg, 111., and Clinton and Cedar Rapids, 10. Urgent in- vitations for his services came from a large number of the leading cities of the United States and Canada—enough, in fact, to occupy his time for at least two years.

===== 0002601_19060929_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
NEXT NATIONAL COUNCIL.

Preparations for the next Free Church Council to be-held hi Leeds in March, 1907, are already proceeding apace. A meeting was held a few days ago, at Leeds, of the Executives of the Leeds Council and Federation for the purpose of taking into consideration the details of arrangements. Rev. Thomas Law attended. The greatest interest was shown in the matter, and everything at this stage is promising well. The sessions and meetings will be held in the Coliseum, and it is intended to hold district meetings in about thirty of the principal places around. The programme in preparation shows considerable variety and interest. A special choir of a thousand voices is being formed to assist in, the vocal part of the proceedings.,

===== 0002601_19060929_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
'DR. CLIFFORD'S BIRTIIDAy.

October will be notable in that on the llith will be celebrated the 70th anniversary of Dr. Clifford's birthday. All who value civil and religious freedom acknowledge the importance of the strenuous battle on its behalf that has been so magnificently waged by our eminent - Free Church , leader' and Dr. Clifford's 70th birthday is not a local but a very national event. It was only fitting that some acknowledgment of his great services should be made, and this is being done by the raising of a fund for the purpose of a joint annuity for Dr. and Mrs. Clifford, together with such smaller gifts as may be subsequently determined. An influential committee has the matter in hand.

===== 0002601_19060929_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
NEW INSTITUTE wonx.

The institutional movement is proceeding apace. In South London a promising start has been made; the Deptford Free Church Council and Young Free Churchmen's League have com- bined in the organising of an Institute and Club. Stonehouso Lodge, 273, Lewisham High-road, has been taken for the headquarters and the opening ceremony was fixed for September 29. Every facility will be afforded to make the Institute an ideal club for the student, the athlete, the social reformer and the Christian worker.

===== 0002601_19060929_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
CHURCH MAGAZINES.

, Although it is yet some time before the New Year season, those who have the management of local Church magazines are already, beginning to look forward to their next year's programme. Some time ago the National Free Church Council started the Free Churchman especially for 9le purpose of supplying a suitable "inset" for such magazines. It is the general opinion that the Free Churchman has improved wonder- fully of late years, and the feet is well known that during the last four years it has more than quadrupled its circulation. For some months strenuous preparations have been put into the programme for 1907, and in a few days advance copies of the January number with complete programme will be, ready. Those who have seen this issue declare that it passes all previous records. Those interested in localising should obtain a copy from the publisher, Memorial Hall, E.C.

WELL-KNOWN HORSE BREEDER DEAD.

_ The death took place at his residence, Berkeley Towers between Crewe and Nantwicb, on Tuesday, of Mr. All Silvester Day. Mr. Day bad for Boma years been a breeder of hackney horses, and was the owner of the well-known Berkeley stud. He was a very success- ful exhibitor, and prominent supporter of Cheshire and neighbouring county agricultural societies.
