LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith.

The Personnel of the Council. am glad to observe a growing opinion that an improvement in the personnel of our Town Council is desirable. It seems to be mainly composed of men whose business experience has been acquired in small retail shops or through dealings in Jerry property. Would not the whole community gain enormously if political and personal rivalries could be buried and men of such unquestioned / ability as Messrs. James Dodds,Henry Roberts,W. P. Rylands, F. W. Monks, L. Greening and George Crosfield were elected to the Town Council? Their experience of great industrial undertakings would be invaluable to the municipality. Mr. A. J. Jolley's efforts to secure better treatment for the sick and aged poor and his wide knowledge of public affairs would make him a valuable member and not the less so because of his courage in setting forth unpalatable truths in plain and forcible language. Last, but not least,tbe names of Messrs. John White and C. E. Parker would complete a list of nine which I suppose would be universally admitted to mark an improve- ment upon the nine whose terms expire on October 31st. Yours respectfully,

C. HEESO3I.

Padgate-lane, September 26th, 1906. -- Baptist Church Harvest Festival. Sir,—A circular bas been issued to the public announc- ing the above services which contains a statement that •4 At the close of June, 1906, we had an accurulated debt upon our church finances of .f.:50." This statement has created the impression that the Rev. H. Dunnico left the church burdened with this debt. As church treasurer at the time Mr. Dunnico concluded his ministry, and made the statement in Parr Hall, that he was leaving the church with sufficient funds to meet all outstanding accounts, I beg to state, that his state- ment was quite true, and was in fact based upon figures supplied by myself to Mr. Dimple° immediately prior to the farewell service.—Yours faithfully, ARTHUR P. FREEMAN. 137, Liverpool-road, Warrington.

Sir,—My attention has been called to a handbill bearing the names of Messrs. B. Few, E. Wynne-Jones and D. Buckby, stating that at the end of June there was an accumulated debt of £.50. I desire to say that when I terminated my ministry on June 24 there was in the hands of the Treasurer more than suacient cash to meet all outstanding accounts. The handbill is incon- sistent with the facts, and lam informed it has been issued without the authority of either Church or Com- mittee, but evqn had it been endorsed by both it would have been none the less misleading.—Yours very sincerely, Kensington Baptist Church, Sept. 26, 1306.

HERBERT DIINNICO.

The Requisition to the Mayor. see that a requisition has been signed by 20 members of the Town Council, calling for a special meeting of the Council to consider the Edelsten Mill purchase. I also notice that the requisition has been forwarded to the Mayor by Mr. James Dodds. Why this procedure? Has our Town Council fallen into such a parlous condition that when twenty of its mem- bers sign a requisition they have to seek outside assist- ance in order to have it forwarded to the proper quarter? Many questions have been asked of late, as to why men of the calibre of some of the members of the Council elected during the last few years should have found it possible to obtain seats on that body. Perhaps the incident of this requisition may shed some light upon the matter. I can quite understand that those who have been chiefly responsible for the election of the majority of this class of member, should have thought it altogether useless to approach gentlemen of ability and independence of character with a view to their becoming candidates, as such persons would doubtless never have consented to occupy the position of mere puppets expected to dance according as the strings happened to be pulled by someone behind the scenes. Is it not time that this game of municipal marionettes should cease and that a class of men should be sent to the Council capable of acting for themselves in the manner they consider best calculated to further the interests'of the town, rather than persons who are content to be used merely as machines to carry out the directions of someone from outside? I was sorry to see some of the names affixed to the requisition (not because of the nature of the requisi- tion itself, with the merits of which I am not dealing), and can only suppose that the owners have temporarily succumbed to the blandishments and pressure brought to been upon them by the henchmen of the manipulator of municipal strings. Warrington has before now displayed a very strong objection to being ruled by an autocrat. I trust that it Is capable of better things than merely to exchange oneclass of autocrat for another, and that its electors will take adeanate steps at the earliest possible moment to show this latest aspirant to the position of arbiter of the town's destinies, municipal and otherwise, that they have just as pronounced an aversion to an auto- cracy supported by means of the pulling of strings from outside the Council as to one based upon individual action in the Council itself.—Yours, ezc., WARRINGTON RATEPAYER.

===== 0002601_19060929_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
The Aim of the IL P.

Sir, —I am in cordial agreement with the sentiment expressed that "to distort facts and make the truth appear false is not the way to secure reforms " or. to put it in the more forcible language of Voiney, "If truth is one and opinions are various it is evident that some are in error. If then such vast numbers of us are wrong who shall dare to say 'I an right.' Let us there- fore be indulgent in our discussions. Let us seek the truth as if no-one 'possessed it." There is no necessity to "distort facts" for the sake of scoring at the expense of an opponent, nor is there any necessity to display unseemly exhibitions of bitterness or animosity on differences of opinion. I think it will be conceded that the Independent Labour man is actuated by con- scientious motives in shaping out a policy that appeals to him as the best in the interest of humanity, justice and right. I regret my reference to "our labour organisations" has been somewhat misunderstood and called forth a passing sneer, but that by the way. I will make another attempt to make my meaning clear to Mr. Morton's vision. This is not the first occasion that the I.L.P. has been accused of endeavouring to capture " our labour organisations" for its own party objects and purposes. And it is a strange fact that the accusation is generally aimed by egregious individuals outside "our labour organisations." I wonder if the charge is levelled with sincerity. To be absolutely frank I deny that it is. But charitably accepting the contention that the studious and diligent progressive politician believes " our labour organisa- tions "to be under the magnetic influence of aggressive LL.P'ers, the thought is excruciating. It looks like a veritable picture on a miniature scale of a modern Spanish inquisition under the folds of the Union Jack. Surely it is a remarkable conception to mist in enlightened minds. A very poor estimate must be entertained in certain quarters of the mental capacity, the sturdy calibre of the organised sons of labour. The ordinary trade unionist possesses sufficient intelligence to conduct the affairs of his union, to prevent " ambushes" or political traps that are inimical to his interests. Mr. Morton is an ardent Liberal for he believes the hope of the worker is the realisation of the principals of Liberalism, but he enters an emphatic protest in his first communication against our purely labour organ- isations been made to serve the ends of one political party—and that the Socialist party." A few sentences below he g,oes on to say " what is practical in its (1.L.P.) teaching is part and parcel of Liberal ism."A little further on he takes advantage of the occasion to strengthen his argument and remarks "indeed the ideal which has the strongest influence in the I.L.P. movement to-day was not generated within that party, hut is the outcome of Liberalism." Why all the fuss and trepidation, why the horrible dread that the T.L.P. will capture our trade unions, our District Council, our Town Council, our House of Commons or our House of Lords; why all the obstacles and barriers placed in the path of the I.L.P. • by our Liberal friends in the laudable effort to educate the masses, capture the legislative machinery and put into operation that "part and parcel of Liberalism" that the originators have so shamefully neglected to perform when the opportunities presented themselves ? Let us recognise the situation. The organised workers and the disorganised proletariat have had driven home to them in no uncertain way that they cannot expect legislation of such a character as will tend to any improvement in economic conditions from the capital- istic classes who have dominated for centuries the government of our country. The Labour party does not engage in the political fight for pastime or amuse- ment. The fight is real, sincere and earnest to them. Their conflict is to remove the hardship of the workers' lot, to make the pressure of daily toil, the struggle for existence easier. There is nothinc higher or nobler on the earth than to serve your fellows. Animated by these ideas, can there be any doubt that by thus !triving the Labour party will do something to make this earth the paradise it was intended to be and can be made? Feeling convinced I have occupied sufficient valuable space already, I leave the other points raised to some other occasion,—Yours truly,

A CASWOREER;

===== 0002601_19060929_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. Morton and the Right to Work Committee.

Sir,—With your perrnissiong wish to reply to several Points in Mr. J. Morton's letters which appeared in the "Examiner" on the 9th and 22nd of September. It WBB not my intention to enter into the discussion until I found that in his last letter your correspondent states that " the Right to Work Committee is nothing more than a decoy' intended to draw unthinkina people into the Socialist party, 8:c." This I deny, and I hope to be able to prove to your readers that the state meat is incorrect.

We have become accustomed to the false charges which are repeatedly being made by your correspondent and his colleagnes against some of us who are both trade unionists and members of the Independent Labour Party. It would appear to be part of their " stock-in-trade " to impute dishonest motives to us. Let me say here that we have no desire to draw unthinking people into the Socialist pazty, rather is it when they have begun to think for themselves that they join the I.L.P. Instead of the I.L.P. having " decoyed" the trade unionists into the Right to Work Committee, permit ma to point out that the I.L.P.is connected with the ceinnaittee on the invitation of the trades unionists. The first step taken towards the formation of a committee in Warrington was owing to letters having been sent to MP as secretary of the Trades Council, from the " Right to Work National Council." Similar letters were sent to all Trades Councils asking them to form local committees to act in conjunction with the National Council. Here follow copies of minutes from the Trades Council's minute book "Correspondence was read from the Right to Work National Council urging this Council to take steps to form a committee in Warrington, and after a lengthy discussion it was unanimously resolved that a conference be convened, and that every trades union branch in Warrington, along with the local Labour Representation Committee and the Independent Labour Party be invited to send delegates." It was further resolved "That these organizations be invited to send delegates in the same ratio as the the representation to the Trades Council." Messrs Plumb and Stott were appointed to represent the Trades Council at the Conference. Acting -under the above instructions I convened af conference which was held in the Spiritualist Hall on March 21th this year. The following societies were repre- sented :—Bakers, Wire Drawers, Amalgamated Joiners, Coach Makers, Painters, Glass Makers, Albion Lodge of Ironworkers, Postmen's Federation. Shop Assistants General Railway workers, Assurance Agents, Engineers, Wheelwrights and Smiths, Enginemen, Trades Council, L.R.C. and I.L.P. Two other branches had appointed delegates who who were unable to be present. In my opinion, the attendance was a fairly representative one from a trade union standpoint, and for Mr. Morton to suggest that these delegates were unthinking persons, other than the two I.L.P. representatives, is a gross insult to the mon 'who were present. It was unanimously resolved by the conference that a "Right to Work Committee" should be formed in Warrington. A discussion then took niece regarding the constitution of the committee. Various sugges- tions were made, but it was the opinion of the delegates that it would be unwise to form another organisation on the lines of the Trades Council and L.R.C. to which all labour organisations cbuld be directly affiliated and to which each affiliated branch would pay subscriptions direct, it being thought that a large number of branches would decline to become members on those lines. Another reason for not attempting to form a large organisation was that the summer moths were approach- ing when unemployment, as a rule, is not very acu' , with a consequent lack of interest in the unemploy question. After a lengthy discussion it ee unanimously resolved to invite the thre organisations to form a "Right to Work Com- mittee " to be composed of fifteen delegates. I ought to add that a number of the delegates were also members of the Progressive Labour League and sup- ported the resolution. On the invitation, therefore, of the conference the organisations named selected five delegates each to constitute the Committee. I ask Mr. Morton, where was the political intriguing by the I.L.P. which he complains of? Even your correspondent himself states in one of his letters "that the trade unions themselves hoisted this new tangled Committee into the political atmosphere Scc.; therefore to be consistent he should blame the trade unionists, if any blame is deserving, for the con- stitution of the Committee, and not the I.L.P. It is quite true that the executive of the Progressive Labour League desired to send delegates to the Committee, and their application was considered at the first meeting. Being desirous of acting in a democratic manner we as a Committee decided to ask the organisations we represented whether they were in favour of the League being permitted to send delegates. The matter was brought before the Trades Council but owing to the fact that at the same meeting a proposal was made that an attempt be made to form one organisation to take the place of the Trades Council, L.R.C. and Right to Work Committee, it was decided to adjourn trie question of admitting other organisations to the latter Committee until the question of unification had been settled. It will be common knowledge to your readers that the latter question has not yet been decided. I therefore ask your readers to judge whether there has been any intriguing or dis- honest practices by the I.L.P. as your correspondent suggests. I can assure Mr. Morton and his colleagues that there is no need for them to "do their best to prevent the I.L.P. from taking ambush in our labour organise- aions." I awaited your correspondents second letter with interest,hoping to learn what he meant by " our labour organizations." However, Mr. Morton deems it inadvisable to tell us. In reply to " Gasworker " he says." no one knows the labour organisations better than a member of the I.L.P. seeing that his party feeds as a parasite on them and would without them have little vitality." I agree with your correspondent that the I.L.P. feeds on Labour organisa- tions. It also feedsl on Liberal and Conservative organisations and has begun to feed on the Progressive Labour League. Is it the latter fact that has begun to alarm Mr. Morton, or does he console himself with the thought that it is only the unthinking members 1 of the League it is feeding on. It is quite true that the feeding gives the I.L.P. vitality, in fact, I venture to suggest that if there was no "feeding " so-called, there would be no Independent Labour Party. Your correspondent tells us that " what is practical in the teachings of the I.L.P. is part and parcel of Liberalism, some of the most extreme items of their programme having been advocated by the Liberal party long before the I.L.P. was born." am not going to deny that statement entirely, but in reply permit me to say that it is because the Liberal party has been aavocatiag them so very long without mak- ing an attempt to put them into practical operation— and they have had ample opportunities—that the Inde- pendent Labour Party was formed. Why has the Liberal Party not carried out the practical part of the I.L.P. teaching ? Had they done this it would not have been left to the I.L.P. to advocate "the practical Liberal proposals." If the Liberal Party refuses year after year to carry out the practical part of our programme which we are told they have been advocating for genera- tions, what hope is there for us to expect them to carry out "some of the most extreme items which have been advocated by the Liberal Party before the I.L.P was born "and which Mr. Morton wishes your readers to believe are not practical. Surely your correspondent's statements are in themselves sufficient to justify the existence of an Independent Labour Party. Your correspondent in his last letter gave us an example of what he calls " the simplest and most direct form of labour representation possible," and asks "could there be a purer form of labour representation than this " ? Evidently, a large number of the members of the Miners' Federation,to which your correspondent refers are not satisfied with the purest.form of labour repre- sentation judging from their expressed desire to join the Labour Party. In conclusion, let me ask Mr. Morton to tell us what labour organizations he means when he writes of" our labour organizations." The inference is that he is a member of a trade union organization. If so, will he tell its which it is. I know Mr. Morton is a member of the Liberal Labour League. He was also a member of the Social Service League, and founder of the Right to Labour League. What has become of the two latter organisations? One would require a very wide stretch of imagination to call the first named a labour organisation. It is gener- ally looked upon as an appendage of the Liberal Asso- ciation, and its policy, as expressed to me, was, prior to the General Election, to secure the return of Mr. Crosfield. and since the election, to prevent the defeat of Mr. Crosfleld, M.P. If that is the policy of the League I have no fault whatever to find with it, but pray don't call it a Labour organisation. Mr. Morton tells us that the League has a membership of 700. What proportion of the 700 pay weekly or monthly con- tributions? Other questions which are interesting,and which Mr. Morton, as secretary, could no doubt answer, are, who pays for the public meetings, the cheap trips, ' the At Homes, and the free tea parties ? Does it comel out of the contributions of the rank and file ?--- Yours, fk.c., FRED STOTT.

HO, Battersby-lane. -- Sir,—After reading Mr. Morton's letter of September 20th, I am compelled to admit that I am totally ignorant of the methods resorted to by him. This of course need not occasion any surprise, for no matter , how clever the conjurer may appear to his audience, they know that he has been " culling" them all the time. It's his business. The people like it, and the ' more skilfully he does the trick the more marked is thier appreciation. I have seen many such tricksters but Mr. Morton is the--latest. I hold the palm up to Mr. Morton on the principle that honour be given where honour is due ! The tactics of Mr. Morton are contemptible. We were discusssng the Right to Work Committee, and my letter of the 14th inst proves con- clusively that Mr. Morton was wrong, but instead of acknowledging his mistake he chides me for my ignor- ance. Heavnos—ignorance! Mr. Morton need not trouble to remind me how little I know, because I I already know. Since his last letter, however, I knoi, a little more, viz. :—Mr. Morton is no mine of informa- tion. I might say more. but what's the use when Mr. Morton will persist in shifting his ground ? He is not man enough to stick to the point in question, but drops it like a hot potato and tries then to quarrel with a chap. ft won't come off. There's an election approach- ing. Now sir, I have finished—at least when I tell Mr. Morton if ever he has occasion to loin the ranks of the unemployed the Right to IVork Committee will fight his cause for 'a that and 'a that. Stay! one word more. "You can fool some of the people some of the time; you can fool some of the people all the time : but you can't fool all the people all the time! " " Break" your stick Mr. Corrp. I've finished till my friend is in abetter humour.—Yours Stc., JAS. HAMPSON. 25, Garibaldi-street, Warrington.

THE PIN-MILL PURCHASE.

CORPORATION SECRETS DIVULGED.

ANOTHER INVESTIGATION COMMITTEE

APPOINTED.

Lively Council Meeting.

Considerable interest centred in a special meeting of the Council, called by the Mayor in response to an extensively signed requisition, which was held at the Town Hall on Wednesday evening. The business as set forth in the requisition was : "To consider the publication in the ` Warrington Guardian' of Saturday, the 15th September, 1906, of a statement with regard to the action pending between the Corporation and Colonel Edelsten and any matter in any way relating thereto, and to pass resolutions thereon." The statement referred to was as follows : MESSRS. EDELSTEN'S WRIT. COUNSEL AND THE CASE FOR THE CORPORATION.

It will be re nembered that at the meeting of the Council, held on August 10th, it was decided that the opinion of connsel should be taken as to the position of the Corporation in the action com- menced by Colonel Edelsten with respect to the compensation to be paid to him for the removal of the Mersey Works in Knutsford-road, for the pur- poses of continuing the widening of that thorough- fare. The Stre 4 Improvement and Lighting Committee passed a resolution to the effect that the Nlayor be empowered to obtain independent professional advice and assistance with regard to his positicn in the action. The opinion of each counsel has been received, and both advise the Corporation to settle and carry out the agreement which had been entered into as beinr, the most economical course to adopt. Several members, however, have expressed a strong opinion in favour ' of continuing the action. I The Mayor (Alderman Bolton) presided over the meeting, and there were also present :—Aldermen Tinnion, Smethurst and Bennett, Councillors Fulton- Smith, Benson, Duncan, Craik, Ball, Wright, Stott, Pemberton, Lord, Chamtler, Dickinson, Wilding, Sankey, Barton, Wilkinson, Dr. Joseph, Magina, Cunnington, James Evans, Skelland, Barton and Ashcroft, .Nlessrs. J. Lyon Whittle (town clerk), A. T. liallaway (deputy town clerk), J. Fairhurst (borough treasurer), and T. Longdin (borough surveyor). The MAYOR intimated that according to the requisi- tion the sole business was to consider the publication of a certain statement in the" Warrington Guardian" and he asked the members to confine themselves to that question and to get the meeting over as quickly as vossible.

--- -:;;• uounciiior LORD : I suppose we must accept your ruling, although I don't agreed with it. I move that we go into committee and that the Press and public be allowed to remain.

The IlaYoß objected to this course and a spirited discussion ensued,. Councillor LORD pointing out that his reason for desiring the Council to go into committee was that the meeting and the subject for discussion were most unusual and some members might therefore want to sneak more than once. Councillor WILDING seconded the proposition which was agreed to.

===== 0002601_19060929_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
"A SERIOUS MATTER."

Councillor LORD said that to his -mind the publica- tion of the statement referred to was a very serious matter for the Council and the town. He did not in any way blame the "Guardian" for publishing the information given to them, but he did blame the person or persons, whoever they might be, who communicated such a serious statement to the Press while the matter was sub judice. They all knew that the matter was practically in the Courts and someone had been good enough, after they had taked counsel's opinion, to com- municate part of that counsel's—opinion which he con- sidered was a most unfair communication. It was, in a sense, not correct. It was a' portion of counsel's opinion calculated to his (Councillor Lord's) mind to prejudice and misguide the to wn,if not the Council. If that had not been the intention of the one who communicated it, he would have given counsel's opinion in full. In fact, it was not counsel's opinion but connsel's advice. He considered that whoever had done this had practically given the Corporation's case away to their opponents, and he thought the Council were now entitled to com- municate to the Press the other side of their case- which would put quite a different construction upon it. He moved that the other side, which was certainly favourable to the Corporation, be now read by the Town Clerk. _ _

The TOWN CLERK : I have not got the opinions here. They are in Liverpool with my agents. Councillor LORD : It is most unfortunate that the Town Clerk has not got the opinions here, seeing that we are called specially together to consider the matter. The TOWN CLERK was understood to ask if it would not be sufficient to state that two eminent King's Counsel had advised that the Corporation had a good defence to the action.

The Mavoa : What they want to know is who gave the information to the " Guardian ?" Councillor LORD : I want the town to krow what counsel's opinion is as to the position of the Council in the matter.

The TOWN CLERK : The whole of it ? Councillor LORD : No. I want the town to know the true facts of the case and they have not got them in the published statement. Whether it was communicated by an official or some privileged member of the Street Improvement Committee I do not know. The Maxon. ; You want it to go the town that the " Guardian's "version is only one side of it. Councillor LORD: I want the Council to know what the true statement of counsel is and the true position of the Council. The TOWN CLERK explained that the reason he had not the opinions there was because a summons was heard in the Courts the previous day and the opinions were requ i red. The .NlAyort : I think it will serve your purpose to let the public know that the " Guardian " has only got one side of the facts.

Councillor WILDING : No, Mr. Mayor, we want to know who gave this information. Councillor Wilding was assailed with cries of "Chair " because ha had addressed the Mayor while remaining seated. The MAYOR : As the " Guardian " won't divulge anything, I don't know how you are going to find out who gave the information. The Press are privileged in their communications. They won't tell you where they get anything from and sometimes they put wrong con- structions on what you give them. You want to know how the " Guardian" got possession of this statement which is only a garbled version. Councillor STorr said those who were at the General Purposes Committee knew perfectly well that opinions had been got from three counsel. These opinions were read to the committee on Monday and he understood that what was complained about—and he felt much annoyed that such a ststment should apposr in the Press at this juncture—was that the opinions of two counsel only had bsen published and the opinion of the third, which was certainly favourable to the Cor- poration, had not been give.. (" No.") The Tows CLERK: The third opinion is that of the Mayor's own private counsel. The M.troa : I have never heard any of them, and know nothing about them.

Councillor-Srorr said one opinion was certainly not published. The Tows CLERK" said he took counsel's opinion from Mr. McMorran, K.C., on behalf of the Corporation,and the Mayor's solicitor (Mr. Longland) took the opinion of Mr. Levet, on his Worships' behalf. The third opinion was that of Mr. Rutherford, of Liverpool, the counsel engaged in the case. Councillor STOTT thought that in all fairness if any information was given to the Press, the complete information should be given. It seemed to him to be an attempt on the part of someone—he did not know whom—to try and prejudice the position of the Council and to defeat those who objected to the purchase of the property. The information ought to have come before the General Purposes Committee or the Council in the 1 ordinary course before anything was given to the Press. I With regard to the case, some members of the Council did not believe that the Council should even acknow- ledge the agreement which had been signed by the Mayor, but that a very great reduction ought to be made in the price or else Mr. Edelsten should be asked to carry out the understanding on which the price was fixed. The information seemed to have been deliberately given to the Press for the purpose of making it more difficult for those who objected to the price. The MAYOR considered it very indiscreet for anyone to give the information to the Press and it was also very indiscreet of the " Guardian" to publish that portion of it. It looked like a keach of confidence on the part of someone. "MORALLY CRIMINAL." Councillor MAGINN quite agreed that it was indis- creet to give the information but he would like to use a stronger term and say that if it was not legally criminal it was morally criminal. That counsel's opinion was the town's property and not that of any individual or of the Guardian." If the Town Council and the officials were anxious to find out who had divulged the information, they could do so. The first onus lay with those few officials who had access to the opinions of counsel and he was perfecfly prepared to take the word of any one of them that he did not give the information. Then, they came to the committee which had the handling of the I affair and the matter would soon narrow itself down until the public would find out who gave the informa- tion. He would go so far as to say that public opinion should be so strongly evidenced against that indi- vidual that he would never have the opportunity of handling the town's affairs or any secrets connected with the Corporation again. What made the question worse still was that the information was published before many members of the Council knew that counsel's opinion had been received and certainly before they had I

" COUNCIL LIKE A HENPEN."

Alderman BENNETT said that if he was one of the members referred to by Councillor Maginn be apologised. He had no intention of interrupting but was simply whispering to his neighbour. He sympa- thised with Councillor Nlaginn, because he knee' it was almost impossible at any time to give utterance to a logical speech at the Council meeting. The Council was more like a benne,' than rmything else —there was continual cackling going on. COUNCILLOR PEMBER,TON MOVES THR APPOINTMENT OF AN INVESTIGATION COMMITTEE. "A DASTARDLY THING."

Councillor PEMBERTON said the Corporation from time to time had had to face many serious questions but this, to his mind, was the most serious position they I had ever been placed in. With regard to the suggestion that a member of the committee might have given the information away, he doubted it. The majority of the Street Improvement Committee were unanimous on a certain course of action. They felt they had not been properly treated in the matter and the majority of the committee took up a certain position which was perfectly right. He did not think it was likely that they would give the case away. He did not blame the " Guardian " because no doubt they innocently gave what they supposed to be the whole opinion. The serious point was that only that part of the opinion had been conveyed which was against the Corporation and it was not the legal part of the opinion' but only the commercial opinion of the barrister. He had very energetically fought against the view expressed by the Town Clerk that the Corporation were bound by the agreement with Mr. Edelsten. He believed that according to the Municipal Corporations Act no agreement could be valid unless the seal of the Corporation was attached to it and no agreement or act of any committee or part of a committee was legal without being confirmed by the Council. He, however, moved that counsel's opinion he obtained for the Corporation and at a subsequent meeting he proposed that counsel's opinion be taken on behalf of the Mayor. The latter was not intended to be for use in the Corporation, but only for the Mayor's protection. The Mayor was a man of commonsense and a shrewd business man, and he (Councillor Pemberton) did not think he would willingly do anything wrong. But if the Mayor did anything on the advice of the Town Clerk of Warrington he (Councillor Pemberton) said that the Town Clerk and not the Mayor was res- ponsible. The opinion of Mr. McMorran, who was the finest K.C. in England on municipal matters, was that there was no legal agreement between the Corporation and Mr. Edelsten. That was definite. When he (Coun- cillor Pemberton) moved that counsel's opinion be taken, he added that the Town Clerk should draw up the cose for presentation to counsel, and lay it before the committee. The Town Clerk did so and he (Conncillor Pemberton) then asked if it would not be better to put in some particulars in regard to the commercial aspect. The Town Clerk, however, said " You are asking an 1 opinion on law" and he (Councillor Pemberton) admitted he had made a mistake in suggesting the introduction of the commercial aspect. But what happened ? Counsel advised on a point of law and then went further and said that in his opinion it would perhaps be better for the Corporation to settle the matter. That part was communicated to the "Guardian." He (Councillor Pemberton) submitted that counsel was wrong because had he (Councillor Pemberton) known that counsel was going to be asked for his view on that point he would have seen that the commercial aspect of the case was put before him. Counsel knew nothing about that. When the majority of the Council passed a resolution that a certain course should be taken' it was a deplorable fact if everybody connected with the Council did not put their backs into the case and fight solid. That was why he had asked time after time for members of the Council to take more interest in the work and have a greater grasp of the Council's affairs and not leave so much to the officials. He had been astounded since he entered the Council to find how little of the facts of Corporation business was communicated to the members. It was the Council's own fault for allowing such a state. of affairs. He had not the slightest doubt that if the officials and everybody connected with the case put their backs into it,the Corporation would come out on top This communication of business to the Press was such a serious matter that he would move that a committee be appointed to inquire into it. If any member of the Council or any official would go so far as to communi- cate a matter of that kind to the Press to be publicly printed, what protection had they against such a man going to an individual and perhaps placing the Corpor- ation in a position to lose thousands of pounds ? If he world dare to communicate business to the Press which published it to the world what would he do when he could go quietly and no one would know,and communi- cate the business of the Corporation to their opponents? It was the most dastardly thing that ever happened in public life and there was not the slightest security for the public if this sort of thing went on. Councillor Witionco seconded the appointment of the committee and suggested that Councillors Lord and Pemberton should be members of it. The MAYOR : We had better do one thing at once. Councillor WILKINSON thought he was right in saying that the opinion of Counsel was given to the General Purposes Committee. Councillor CRANTLER : Not before it was published. Mr. Hari, k way : It was given to the General Purposes Committee for the first time on Monday. Councillor Wrm:rxsox : I was under the impression that it was given in the Council chamber last week. Continuing be urged that it would be foolish, because a paper had given a garbled report, for the Council to publish the whole of counsels' opinion and so give away the whole of their case.

Con- tinuing, he said the opinion which had been published, VMS false and altogether wrong, and he thought the best thing tbev as a Council could do was to repudiate it entirely. It seemed to him that it was an attempt on the pert of their opponents to draw from them their real position, and he thought that if they were going to give any more of the opinions, they were going to give their case away entirely. From what he knew he believed the Corporation had a very strong case indeed. (Hear, hear.) He judged that from the opinions which were read on Monday, and therefore he would repudiate what had been published and let the public know that they had con- fidence in their case. If the object of the meeting was to remove any false impression which had been created by the statement in the " Guardian" then the best thing they could do was to ignore that statement. He thought that considerable harm might have been done by the divnl ging of the information but it was not the first time within his recollection that information had been divulged. He had known business of a committee meet- ing to be published within half an hour from the Parr Hall platform. If they formed a committee there might be some other matters for it to investigate, but perhaps if they loaded it too much they would not finish before the first of November. It seemed to him that they had a silly season in Warrington just before the November elections. All those peculiar things arose and all those statements were made, which might be for the good of the town or might serve another purpose. He agreed with the appointment of the committee as he thought it might probably get at the name of the informant and at the probable object and reason why the information was given. Moreover he thought it would put them in a position that they were not likely again to suffer from a similar act on the part of any official or member of the Corporation. He would not use any unnecessary adjectives with regard to the character of the person who gave the information but it would not strengthen the hands of the Corporation to give the case away entirely and he understood that their case was a particularly strong one. He thought their friends in calling that meeting might have been able to put a finger upon the party ar parties who had given the information. There had really been so much of what one gentlemen described as " spitting in each other's ear-holes " at the street corners and in the committee rooms and he thought the detectives had investigated the whole case and were ready to put their hands upon the culprit and if necessary to commence proceedings. He hoped, however, that the Committee would be able to unravel the mystery. _ .

Their friends were continually finding mare's nests and would go on finding them until the end of the chapter. Councillor PEMBERTON It is not insinuation it is in the Press.

Alderman BENNETT continuing, said charges had been made against members of the Council and the officials, most exaggerated charges, but practically nothing had I been proved to justify them. In the report of the great inquiry there was practically nothing except what was common knowledge. It was continually insinuated that information was withheld, that there was practically a conspiracy of silence and that they were banded together to keep things in the dark. That was entirely incorrect, but he thought there was a desire to use the cry for electioneering purposes. Councillor PEMBERTON : You are an alderman. Alderman BENNETT : I am, but I am not afraid of fighting some members of the Council. Councillor PEMBERTON : Then retire and fight me. Alderman BENN'ETT : lam not going to do so. I would chose a foeman worthy of my steel. Councillor PEMBERTON: Any excuse is better than none. Alderman TINNION moved that the meeting resolve itself into a Council meeting. Councillor WILKINSON seconded.

Councillor PBMBERToN : I object. Councillor EVANS rose to a point of order, but the Mayor refused to hear him and threatened to leave the chair. Councillor Evans withdrew, and the motion was put to the meeting and defeated. On a vote being taken it was unanimously decided to appoint a committee to consider the question. Some difficulty arose as to the constitution of the committee, several members declining to serve. Councillor PEMBERTON wished to reply at length to Alderman Bennett, but the Mayor refused to allow him to do so. Alderman BENNETT : I do not deny that there were certain items in the report but they were practically common knowledge before the report was issued. Councillor CRA.IK suggested that they should oppoint a permanent investigation committee. Dr. lOsEPIL was asked to serve on the committee but declined. Councillor Pemberton, he said, had stated that he did not do any work in the Council and he was going to stick to that. A lively passage at arms occurred between the doctor and Councillor Pemberton, the doctor telling Councillor Pemberton that he ought to tell the truth and that he did as much work as Councillor Pemberton. Councillor BURToN : I think we have had enough of this tomfoolery. Ultimately Councillors' Pemberton, Wilding, Barton, Wilkinson, Magian, Evans, and Ball were elected to form the Committee. _ . _

The meeting then resolved itself into a Council meeting and the resolutions of the Committee were confirmed.

Bowsey-road Wesleyan Church, preachers, 10.30, Rev. W. Mellor ; 6.30, Rev. S. Pitt. Golborne-street Baptist Church (harvest festival), preacher, 10.30 and 6.30, Rev. J. T. Schofield. Soloists Miss E. Cairo-street Unitarian Church (harvest festival), preacher, 10.30 and 6.30, Rev. C. Harvey Cook. Wycliffe Church, preacher, 10.30 and 6.30, Rev. J. Yonge. ST. JAMES', LATCIIFORD. The harvest festival commenced on Wednesday even- ing when a large congregation assembled. The church, which has recently been renovated and re-decorated, was very prettily embellished with flowers, fruit, corn Sm., the work having been undertaken by the following: Miss Burgess, Miss A. Parry, Mrs. Fargher, Miss Eckerley, Mrs. Green, Mrs. Booth, Mrs. Grant and the Vicar. The service 'was of a joyous character, the anthem "Great is the Lord " (Dr. Marchant) being ren- dered with taste and expression. A pleasing feature was a quintette by A. Hill, W. Foulkes J. Pevitt, J. Skelhorn and J. Hill. Mr. E. Davies pre- sided at the organ. The preacher was the Rev. J. Wakeford, vicar of St. Margaret's, Anfleld, who based a powerful discourse on ti-e words : "Then whose shall those things -be which thou hash provided ? (Luke xii. 20). He dvelt upon the fleeting and unsatis- fying nature of earthly joys and successes and appealed to his hearers to turn their thoughts to the things which concerned their eternal welfare. He also emphasised the duty of making the right use of gifts which God had bestowed and was particularly severe on wastefulness, whether of time, money or food. One lesson which he sought to drive borne was that after providity, for our temporal needs the money which remained' must not he wasted, but should be regarded as belonging to God, and used as a trust for him. The misuse of wealth called forth his strong condemnation, and he incidentally remarked that no man could make a million of money honestly. He expressed his alanorrence at the action of certain rich men who joined oogether to invest the profits from a previous successful venture with the avowed intention of making another 15 per cent. He held that the poor had a claim on the surplug,

PEARSON & KNOWLES LTD.

FOREIGN COMPETITION NOT SO

KEEN.

COMPENSATION BILL CRITICISED.

The thirty-third ordinary general meeting of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co. Ltd., was held at the Company's offices, Warrington, at noon on Thurs- day. Mr. J. S. Harmood Banner, M.P. (chairman of directors), presided and there were also present : Messrs. William El. Bleckly (deputy-chairman), John J. Bleakly, George F. Pearson, Richard Brancker, W. Peter Rylands (directors), Jamas Dodds (secretary), and several shareholders. TETE REPORT. The Directors reported that the profits for the year ending June 30th, amounted to £62,806 15s. Bd., and the balance brought forward from last year was £B,OBl 11s. 41., together £70,918 7s. An interim dividend at the rate of 6 per cent per annum on the preference shares and 4 per cent on the ordinary shares was paid in March last for the belt-veer ending December 31st, 1905, amounting to £19,325. The Directors now recom- mended that for the past half-year, a dividend at the rate per annum of 6 per cent on the preference shares and 6 per cent on the ordinary shares be declared.making a total distribution for the year of £44,750. The I Directors further recommended that £lO,OOO be added to the Reserve Fund, that the sum of £6,874 19s. 46., which represented the expenditure on capital account for the year, be written off, and that the balance of £9,323 7s. Bd. be carried forward. During the past year the output of collieries had been well maintained, but prices had continued to rule on a very low basis. In [ the iron and steel trades the works had been fully I employed, and a higher range of prices had been estab- lished. A rise in wages and raw material had,however, , fully counterbalanced this advantage. The engineering trade had been busy in all departments, and the orders land contracts on hand held out a prospect of full work for some time to come. Important additions and 'developments had taken place in this branch of the Company's business. THE CHAIRMAN'S SPEECH. FOREIGN COMPETITION. THE COMPENSATION BILL.

The CHAIRMAN said that in movinr, the adoption of the report and statement of accounts he would like first to refer to the retirement of the senior member of their Board, Mr. Beck. It was a matter of great regret to the directors, and he waT sure the announcement would be received with equal regret by the shareholders who were present. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Beck had been a member of the Board since the forma- tion of the company, and had been of great service, particularly in the engineering department which he had superintended during the last five years conjointly with Mr. Herbert Bleckly. Mr. Beck had retired in consequence of advancing years and he the Chairman) felt sure the shareholders would join with the directors in wishing him a long and pleasant life of ease. (Hear, bear.) While a resolution on the matter would be moved later, he might mention that Mr. Beck had written to the Board suggesting that Mr. Herbert Blackly should be appointed a director in his place. The Board were quite of opinion that it was right and fitting that the engineering department should be represented in their counsels and were prepared to accept the nomination of Mr. Herbert Blackly when approved by the shareholders. The accounts showed a considerably increased liability to creditors-267.,616 as against .217,922 last year. That was an consequence of the very heavy amount of stock they had had to purchase in anticipation of the fulfilment of orders on hand. If the shareholders looked at the reserve fund (2120,000) they would see it had again increased and would be further increased in accordance with the resolutions of the meeting that day. They fortunately had only those two items on the credit side of the balance sheet besides the profit and loss, as they had no bills payable to debentures or any other liabilities of any sort whatever. On the property and assets side of the accounts, they would observe that the freehold and leasehold land &c., stood at about the same figure— . 2561,406, although they had expended during the year on additional reachinery and plant a considerable sum and had also written off all their removals. The shareholders would be pleased to hear that, in addition to the new plant for wheels and axles, the direttors had taken steps to put in electrical machinery in many parts of the works and it was now in active working order and was proving of great advantage to the firm in simplicity and economy. The reserve fund investment account-281,660 against £72,599 last year—had been further increased by the purchase of shares in a concern with which they were in close alliance. The purchase had been of the greatest benefit to them in creating a continuity of production and the concern itself had been worked on a good basis of profits. They had every reason to believe that the advantage of purchase would continue during succeed- ing years. This year they had received a.dividend at the rate of 6 per cent, per annum on the investment which they thought was a better dividend than they would have got if they had invested their reserve fund in consols. The stock was considerably increased, stand- ing this year at £263,000 against £226,000 last year. It had been taken at a low valuation and he thought he might say, below cost. Under the present conditions of trade it was incumbent upon them to take large quantities of raw materials into their works for which they paid cash, differing to a considerable extent from former methods of trade in connection with the supply of raw material. The advantage of their strong posi- tion enabled them to buy in the cheapest market and on the best terms, and therefore they did not suffer as much as they would otherwise do by the lock-up in cash required for the purchase of so large a stock of materials as was represented in the balance sheet. The profit amounted to £62,866 as against a profit last year of 1.257,186, or an increase of .£5,660. This amount of profit had been arrived at after providing generously for all removals and requisite depreciation. This year the iron trade had supplied the greater part of the profit, There was not so very much difference between the two departments, but he mentioned that because it showed a reversal of the position last year when the coal trade produced the greater part of the profit. Coal had not shown that elasticity in profits which they ought to have expected from the prosperity of Lancashire in the cotton trade and also in the iron trade. There bad been

I GREAT DEAL or SHORT TIME during the year and perhaps they would agree with him that that arose to some extmat from the extreme heat. One great difficulty in the coal trade was to arrange the proper quantities of slack and coal. The Lancashire coal trade was also at the present time considerably handicapped by the competition of slack from Yorkshire districts which sold their slack at very low prices at the pit and with cheap railway carriage came into the Lancashire markets to compete. The Yorkshire pits were in many instances new and of great capacity for winding large quan- tities of coal at very cheap rates and whenever the demand from the Continent relaxed there was a heavy pressure from Yorkshire to put coal and slack in the Western or Lancashire market. It was to be hoped that

TUE REMOVAL OF TIM 1. DUTY might improve the demand for coal from the Continent and thus remove the competition which they now had from Yorkshire. One great indication of the state of the coal trade in Lancashire was that the prices of coal supplied to the railways had been at unfavourable rates, or, he should say,at not-increasing rates, the keen competition which they ought to avoid having prevented their obtaining the increase which had been obtained uni- versally in the Yorkshire and Durham districts where •the colliery proprietors seemed to have kept well together. With regard to the iron trade, the rains in New Zealand and Australia and consequent better harvests, had improved their trade and India had co-operated. In the Argentine also they had benefitted by the great prosperity, though they came second-hand in any improvement in that country, as Germany and the United States had the first call. There was on doubt that India and the Colonies were the most valuable of their customers, and when they had good harvests the firm were bound to share in their prosperity. At the same time they felt keenly the competition, even in those markets, which came from foreign conntries—(hear, hear)—and whilst he did not want to refer more than necessary to the question of

COLONIAL AND -INDIAN lIREFERENCE he would like their shareholders and workpeople care- fully to watch the course of trade and to help them to obtain any preference from the Colonies and India which would alleviate the very heavy expenditure on rates and taxes which manufacturers in this country had to incur. He would like to refer to the way in which their compensation was managed. The colliery com- pensation was managed from the Northern Employers' Indemnity Association and the iron trade through their own works. The Northern Employers' Association was very carefully conducted nod from experience and from the views of those who knew the workmen's feelings, he could say there was a through sympathy between the workmen and the employers in their district and there I was no cause for complaint. As regarded the compensa- tion at the iron works, the payment was left in the hands of representatives of the workmen and the firm I found such money as was required to pay in respect of accidents. That, he thought, worked satisfactorily both to the workmen and to the firth and they were indebted to the workmen's representatives who managed it so Iwell. (Hear, hear.) .He was afraid in future they i would have to face some considerable INCREASE IN THEIR COMPENSATION BILL I and particularly in law charges which would arise in ; consequence of the new Act which was being considered by Parliament. He thought he might say for all pre- sent and for other employers he knew of, that they all accepted universally the doctrine that all classes ought tto be included in the Compensation Bill. (Hear, hear.) The advantages which were given to their workmen in the coal and iron trades ought to be given to all classes of employees in the country. In this way he thought the employers went further than the Government who excluded the workmen of small employers, as well

naarine,ts! as domestic servants, hotel waiters, master land co-operative workers in small ships. Personally esn° I saw no reason for the exclusion of any of those c 1 as! and he thought they were equally entitled to the.llT. benefit of the Acts which ordinary workmen recerra He would emphasise the fact that from his know fly of the employers of the country, they were 6'4l'3' anxious that that should be so. He could not letti; the subject without referring to what he considered t NARROW VIEWS or THE LABOUR MEMBERS erg of Parliament. For one thing, there penalty on a workman for refusal to allow a doctel, examination, so that a workman,could,after an accideft. decline to allow the doctor to examine him to 111 thin whether the injury had arisen from an acciden ‘,„d the works or was one of old standing, which been re-opened. He thought it desirable for the holders to know that not by the inclusion of workmen but by the very onerous and unfair regulstlo, which would be imposed on employers by the nellift, would their burden be increased. In conclusion Banner said they were greatly indebted to their otafre, in the coal and iron trades for the work they had deSi, Their sales were,he believed,a record—the total sales it coal and iron amounting to £1,305,000—50 that th,eto officials had had a very considerable amount of won. do. BLECKLY AND "TARIFF REFOilg:' Mr. W. H. BLECKLY seconded. After referring ht: the improved state of trade he said the swing of ' political pendulum had unfortunately favoured, „ Government which refused to assist them in fight'a; the unfair foreign competition to which he had beav drawing the attention of that meeting for so roal years.
